Editor’s preface

Jeff Cameron

Steven Schrembeck was my friend and my mentor. He was a principal engineer at Cox Automotive, a writer, a maker of audio fiction, and the person I trusted most to tell me whether a piece of work was any good. The first time I asked him a question, about machine learning, he answered within the hour with his own code and a list of doors not to knock on. He died in September 2026.

In his last year he made a podcast called Reality 101. On it he says, plainly and with a lot of jokes, that lights in the sky cracked the worldview he had built his life on; that he then taught himself to do things he used to make fun of; that he came to know people who are not physical, among them a sister and a mother; and that one night at the end of December someone showed him how reality works. He says anyone can do what he did, and he spends hours explaining how.

I am an engineer. So was he, and a better one. He describes himself before all this as “a person who is very orderly and logical”, and that is the person I knew. So I can’t wave this away as the sort of thing that sort of person says. I can’t just believe it either. What I can do is take it seriously, which is all he asked of anyone.

I have three reasons for making this book. I’ll give them in the order of how easy they are to admit.

The first is other people. He made the series so that, as he puts it, “people can understand their own reality and make sense of it, but also learn how to do it yourselves”. If something like this is happening to you, I want you to have his account whole. I also want you to know how many other people, across how many centuries, have come back describing the same country.

The second is that I want to understand what happened to my friend, so that I can work out how to keep what he started growing.

The third is selfish. If he is right, he isn’t gone, and he left instructions. One of his last recordings ends: “give me a shout one day. Maybe I’ll hear you.” I want to know how.

Two voices

His words are the body of every chapter. Nothing has been added to them, and the next section explains how that was checked. My pages are set apart and carry my name. In them I do three things.

I narrate: where we are in his story, and what to watch for.

I connect: who else has reported what he reports, from antiquity to last year, with the sources so that you can check me. For each comparison I try to say plainly whether the resemblance is close or loose, and whether he had read the thing I am comparing him to, because a man who has read a book and then has the experience in it is a different kind of evidence from a man who hasn’t.

And I speculate, under headings that say so.

What I don’t do is tell you it is true. I don’t know. He had a rule for this, which I have tried to keep: “I insist on testing weird stuff, but also accountability to skepticism and humility and some openness at the same time, I think.” He called it math and magic.

You should also know that I had help from an AI system: in cutting the transcripts, in finding and checking the sources, and in drafting my own pages. A program verifies every line of his against the transcripts of the recordings. The judgments are mine, and so are the mistakes.

He ended his episodes the same way each time. I’ll borrow the line to begin: “nothing is impossible, you are not going to die, and everything loves you”.

Let’s see.

How this book was made

Reality 101 is a podcast series Steven Schrembeck made with his friend Joshua Swenson. Its episodes were published between May and October 2026. Steven called it “a journal of impossible experiences.” He died in September 2026.

This book holds the whole series in the order he released it, the three solo audio journals he added as bonus episodes, and selections from four interviews he gave before the series began. Around those chapters are pages written by his friend Jeff Cameron, who edited the book.

His words

Every sentence in the body of a chapter was spoken by the person it is attributed to. The text was made from transcripts of the recordings by cutting only. Filler, stutters, false starts, repeated explanations, cross-talk, recording logistics and show promotion were removed. Punctuation, capitalization and paragraph breaks are editorial. No word was added, replaced or moved; where Steven says “gonna,” the page says “gonna.” A program checks each paragraph against the transcript, word by word and in order, every time the book is built.

Chapter titles follow the episode titles. Section headings are editorial. The few explanations placed between passages are labelled *Editor’s context*. Joshua Swenson’s questions and reflections, and those of the interview hosts, appear under their own names, shortened by the same method.

The series and the journals are nearly complete: they keep what Steven said and remove how speech stumbles. The interviews are selections. What was left out of each chapter is described under Sources and editorial note at the back.

The editor’s pages

The preface, the short introductions before the chapters, the pages headed connections and questions after them, and the essays in Part Four are Jeff Cameron’s writing, not Steven’s. They are set apart from the chapters and marked with his name. When they quote Steven, the quotation is checked by the same program against the chapter it comes from. When they draw on another author, the source is listed under Works cited. They were drafted with AI assistance.

What to keep in mind

The transcripts were made by machine and have not been fully checked against the audio by a listener. A machine can mishear a word, and a cut can change an emphasis. Where a transcribed word looked wrong, it was left as transcribed or the clause was left out; it was not replaced with a guess. The spelling of a small number of names and terms was corrected, and each correction is recorded. A list of about two hundred places where the transcript looks doubtful, with the time of each in its recording, is kept with the book’s files for anyone willing to listen and check. Anything that matters to you is worth hearing in his voice: every chapter’s recording and full transcript are in the Steven Schrembeck Archive, linked from the notes at the back.

This edition was prepared after Steven’s death. He did not review or approve it.

The chapters are his accounts of his own experience. They neither argue for those accounts nor against them, and they keep his doubts beside his certainty. The editor’s pages do have a point of view, and say where it is.

How to read it

Start with the Invitation and the Prologue; he called the prologue required listening for everything after it. Parts One and Two then follow his own order. Part Three steps back to the interviews he gave before the series, mostly to people who had asked him about technology. Part Four is the editor’s.

A reader’s guide at the back gathers some of the statements he returned to, an index of the practices he describes for trying it yourself, and a list of the experiences he recounts, each linked to its place in the text.

Invitation

The series trailer

Steven: My name is Steven Schrembeck.

Joshua: And my name is Joshua Swenson.

Steven: So in 2022, a series of UFO sightings forced me to completely reevaluate everything I thought I knew about how reality worked, and I began obsessively researching, training, learning literally everything I could in a desperate reaction to just understand my reality. So I spent years seeking answers to the kinds of questions that nobody actually expects to get answers to. Are we alone? Why am I here? Am I going to die? How does reality itself work? But I did it as a sort of need to make sense of what was happening.

Imagine my surprise when I actually get a reply to pretty much all of them. Clear, direct, testable answers. We are presenting those answers as I experienced them, how I got to them, what the implications are for our reality, and more importantly it’s everything I know about how to do it yourself, because I fundamentally believe that anyone can do this too. And if it’s true, it should be verifiable.

Joshua: I’ll be holding space for this incredible exploration, occasionally playing the skeptic and fellow explorer, pushing back, helping Steven go deeper, trying things out myself, and reporting back.

Steven: Crazy as it may seem, nothing is impossible, you are not going to die, and everything loves you. So please join us for Reality 101, where we try to demonstrate that to you.

Part One The Series

Six conversations with Joshua Swenson, in the order Steven released them.

Prologue

From Ruptures and Roots

Jeff Cameron · before you read

He called this episode zero and said it was required listening. It was recorded for Joshua Swenson’s podcast Ruptures and Roots before there was a series; the archive’s copy of that first release is dated February 24, 2026.

Watch the order of events, because it is not the order I would have guessed. Meditation comes first, taken up in order to be happy. Then the sightings. Then, in late 2024, things in his own head that he could not account for. And then, before any of the training, he does what a careful person does and tries to rule himself out: “I would love for this to be insanity. That would be great.” The psychiatrist he sees tells him the worst thing he could do is suppress it.

He also tells you how to read everything that follows: “This story is not really my story so much, it’s a template.”

Steven: Hey everyone. So the format of this first episode is a little bit different because it’s actually the first episode that my friend and I, Joshua Swenson, who co-hosts this limited series, we ever did, and it worked so well that we decided to follow up with this show. So Joshua was kind enough to allow me to cross-post this first interview, which has all the important context that you’re gonna need to understand the rest of Reality 101. So from here, everything after this episode is slightly different format. Enjoy.

Welcome to the circle

Joshua: Welcome to the circle. This is Ruptures and Roots, and I’m your fellow traveler, Joshua Swenson. This is a space for deep listening and the sharing of stories of rupture and transformation. Sitting in the circle with me today is Steven Schrembeck. I first met Steven through a class I joined last fall called Techniques of Transcendence. I was immediately struck by his curiosity and tenacity in exploring the edges of consciousness. I’ve loved hearing Steven reflect on his encounters with anomalous phenomena and the ways he’s learned to listen to and work with psychic and intuitive capacities.

Steven is a lifelong tinkerer with interests ranging from sensory substitution to societal incentives. By trade, he’s a software engineer, and by avocation, a writer and explorer of meaning. He’s the author of horror short stories and fantasy novels, and he also writes nonfiction on intuition, consciousness, extrasensory abilities, and how we make sense of the unknown at differentcompass.ink. In this conversation, we sit together in curiosity, exploring rupture, perception, and what happens when our understanding of reality begins to stretch beyond its familiar bounds. Welcome to the circle today, Steven. Is there anything more you’d like to say or share by way of introduction?

Steven: You said it way better than I would say it myself, so I better not.

Joshua: Before we open the space for your story, I always like to check in if you’d like to share what’s alive for you today, what’s on your heart and mind, how you’re arriving in the space.

Steven: I arrived poring through some old journals about what’s happened to me over the past few years, and I knew that it was time to record with you ’cause I heard a really high-pitched sound and felt that sort of pressure in my head and went, “Oh, cool. Guess it’s time to go record.” Sure enough, I look up like, “All right. Who needs an alarm?” So that’s how I’m arriving. How are you?

Joshua: I’ve similarly been noticing high-pitched noises. It’s not quite the same intensity as tinnitus, but it comes and goes at unique moments. I was reading a text you sent once about it coming up for you at one point, and as I was reading the text, that ringing came in my own ears.

Steven: Sorry.

Joshua: No, it’s interesting. It feels to me like a signal of synchronicity. So thank you for sharing, and thank you for arriving today.

Steven: Let’s see how many listeners get it at that exact same time right now.

Joshua: I’d like to invite us both to take a breath before we begin. And whenever you’re ready, what was life like before the rupture or wherever it feels right to begin?

Before the rupture

Steven: I think I would like to preface this with how hilarious and ironic it is that I would be describing my life before and after a rupture. As we’ll get into any sort of supernatural occurrence or mystical experience, you’d have to know me, that, like every child, I had magical thinking, but at a pretty young age, I don’t know, nine, 10, start to figure out, “Oh, that’s not cool.” And from that, more likely, others will judge me or, “Oh, this isn’t real. I gotta shut that down.” And I think I did that very successfully.

But I never stopped liking stories and fantastical worlds. That’s not unique among humans, but in high school, I started writing creative fiction. I loved humor, absurdist humor, fantasy, and then eventually I would get into horror. That was an escape, and so was reading, and I never stopped loving fictional magical worlds, but I think I needed a world that made sense to me. I became logical. I liked things that made sense.

A lot of this would end up being coping mechanisms for ADHD, various other acronyms that are useful blueprints, and I wouldn’t find that out until much later in life. But nonetheless, it affected my social life, and it helped me to see the world in terms of systems and functions and things that happen because of reasons, in ways that can be predicted and modeled. 12-year-old Steven wasn’t using those words, but at the same time, we’re clever. We find ways around our problems. Why am I hyperactive? Why do I just blurt things out without saying things? Why can’t I sit here? Why am I so slow on these tests? We find ways around all those things, whether we realize it or not.

And so I had evolved into a person that was fascinated with the natural world, was also fascinated with very separate fictional worlds, some of which I was creating. I loved science. I went to college to become a software developer. I never thought I would get into that college. I never thought I would be a software developer. I didn’t think I was smart enough. Most of my life, I didn’t think I was smart enough, which was counter to what I was told, which was very difficult for me.

What I hadn’t realized until much later on is that I had some basic learning disabilities and that I was actually very slow at thinking. Had my thought process measured, and I’m well below average in terms of how slow I think. But I’ve got a pretty good verbal intelligence, so I can just kinda make stuff up until eventually my brain catches up. But it explained why I felt like I understood things, but I struggled so much. And in a school setting that was very rigorous and had timed things, I was just slow, and I mistook being slow for being dumb. But that wouldn’t hit me until later.

All that to say is that I’m a person who is very orderly and logical, and I’ve just straight up made fun of anything I didn’t understand that I felt was well outside the realm of materialism, even when I didn’t use those words. Reason. I felt that everything could be explained. If you can know the state of everything, and you had just enough understanding of how it all worked, you could predict everything forever, right? I had that belief as recent as six years ago, maybe five years ago. So out of the important context, that’s who I was.

Later in my adult life, I would come to call myself an atheist, and it felt right. I never liked religion. I still don’t. I don’t dislike people who are religious, but it does not feel right for me. I didn’t like dogma. I never liked being told what to do or what to think or what I couldn’t ask. That bothered me a lot. I always liked questions, and I’ve always had an accountability to truth, which led me to questioning religion and a lot of things. I became vegan. We parent our children in pretty unusual ways. We don’t go to a traditional schooling system. We do a lot of things that are not normal and weren’t easy to make those choices, but it felt right.

So that’s what life was like before a rupture for me. I had systems, I had rules, I thought in structure. I had a spreadsheet that I reviewed every morning of here’s who I wanna be, here’s how I work. I had a whole life that was architected to keep me together and keep everything working. I got pretty good at it, but it was still so much work, just to be happy. I can pause there, but that leads me into, I guess, the beginning of change.

The kid thing

Joshua: I resonate a lot with what you’ve shared, and I really relate to that escape into fantasy. I remember I didn’t feel in my skin or comfortable with my relatives, and anytime we would go to visit them, I would just read Animorphs and fantasy novels and things like that, and I would always get in trouble for wanting to stay in the room and read. But it felt safer for me. And the way you framed it was like this imaginal realm that children are more in touch with.

Imaginary friends, we think of them as not real. For children inhabiting that space, it is real. Their imagination is so much more active. They’re just constantly role-playing and playing and pretending. But we think of it as pretending, right? But they are really in that world, and at some point, that begins to be shut down by all the voices around us and society telling us, “You need to grow up. You need to be serious.” Kids are just in this realm that are more in touch with maybe the ground of reality, like consciousness.

Steven: Sure. Well, I think we’ll come back to the kid thing later ’cause I think this is a common belief that especially in spiritual circles or a lot of these extrasensory perception circles, there’s a belief that children are better. There’s this weird diminishment of adults and older people, which I’m happy to say is not true. It’s not fundamental, right? It’s correlated, but not causative. I didn’t get rid of statistics and logical thinking. I’ve just incorporated it now into a bigger worldview.

Joshua: We could take a minute on that if you wanna go deeper on that. That’s a really good point.

Steven: Sure. One of the ways I know this is true is that children just take the world as it is. They’re extremely malleable, but they’re also sponges, right? We need our world models to function in the world. The world could not be run by children. It would fall apart. It wouldn’t work. We need adult selves, and I think the real goal is to have the capacity to shift in and out of being childlike when it’s appropriate, and that should be the real goal, is to adult and wear pants and do taxes when you have to, but then also be able to dance and not care and have fun and see the world as it is.

And this can be shown. There are a number of psi abilities, so extrasensory abilities that we’ll get into, that people thought only kids could do. And in fact, the only instructors, which there weren’t very many, would only teach children because they just straight up thought adults couldn’t do it. And I’m happy to report that is absolutely not the case. And there is no real difference between the performance between children and adults, especially when you don’t tell the adults that kids are better at it and that adults are bad at it. If you leave all that out and just tell them they can do it, they get basically the same performance, which I thought is very inspiring. It has nothing to do with being a child or not. It’s just that, yeah, they don’t have as much baggage. That’s for sure.

Joshua: There’s a phrase that came to mind, I think this is from Ken Wilber. It’s a jargony phrase, but it’s essentially saying that children are in this flow state, very expressive. But they are not enlightened. You have to go through that journey of losing touch with that state, going through this process of self-identification in order to transcend the ego, right?

Steven: I mean, they also hit people who take their toys and pee themselves.

Joshua: Yeah, I know. I have two young ones also.

Steven: There’s downsides to being a child, you know?

Joshua: I think just in this context of being in touch with the imaginal that I was witnessing in your story, and that I resonate with, in our culture, there’s this idea that that is not as valid as the logical way of being. You forget that and you grow up and you become a serious, focused, rational adult. In a lot of circles, that’s what it means to grow up and mature in our society.

Steven: Well, I think the maturation extends from there. I do think it’s mostly true. Gotta learn how to do that. And that parallels this arc perfectly.

Meditation and being happy

Steven: Okay, so towards rupture, there isn’t a single break. I’ve broken a bunch of times over and over again, and there’s a few that stand out and we’ll get to those. A lot of things happened to me that I can’t explain. They happen with increasing frequency and magnitude, and we’ll get into some of those. But it all started, I’d say two thousand twenty-two, maybe ‘21, is when I started getting very serious about meditation and being happy.

I wrote down here that I was very reactive, hurried. I felt like I was never doing enough. I was very controlling. The way I maintained stability was through systems and control, and it was oppressive, right? It was a system that had to be maintained of self, emotional control, relations, everything, right? And if I fail, it falls apart. So it’s difficult. I got very good at understanding the mind and motivation, emotions and humans, but you can only understand something so much by taking it apart into pieces and building tools and systems. At some point, you reach a limit.

I was effective, but I was unhappy, or it was difficult to maintain it, and I hated it, and I wanted a way to be happy. Just by default, I wanted to not have to try so hard all the time. And that led me, as with many people, to schools of Eastern thought, and I started to get very serious about meditation. And that is when I started setting a daily habit of twice a day for thirty minutes, which would eventually turn into an hour, and then now it’s closer to two hours a day of meditation. But I’ve kept it up since then. In terms of recorded meditations, I’m near six hundred, but many of them never got written down.

And that is when things started to change. So I started doing those meditations, and I had tried, like many people, to do it as a prescriptive thing. “Oh, it’s de-stress.” This is like a thing I have to do, like exercise. And now it was beginning to be about self-exploration. And I think what really kicked it off was the book, The Untethered Soul. Just an incredible piece of work. If you could only read one book, oh, that’s a good one.

I realized, oh, wait a minute, you can use meditation for more than just feeling better. And it’s more than just meditation, right? You carry these practices throughout your life. It’s a way of seeing the world. So I start doing it like, “Oh, you can do inner exploration.” What does that even mean? And so I’m like, “I want that experience.” I thought you could only get this with drugs.

And so I start doing it, and I start to have fascinating experiences, which are mostly a sense of floating or zooming or shifts in consciousness. Nothing extraordinary, something that anyone could get with thirty minutes of serious meditation. But now I’ve brought a sense of intention to this, and that has changed everything. And I want more of it. I don’t just feel de-stressed. I feel like a different person. My second half of my day now is, I’m less reactive. I feel more free. I don’t feel like I have to do things in the same way. I feel like stuff is just coming to me easier. And this becomes pretty good motivation to keep exploring. I’ll pause there if you have any questions, but that’s really what kicks it off.

Joshua: I relate to that. A shift in meditation. I’ve been meditating for about twenty years now, but for the first thirteen, fourteen years of that time, it was really this focus training when you’re breathing. If you had noticed a thought, come back to your breath, don’t think, just be present, pushing away emotion, pushing away thought. And for me, there was a big shift when I began to become more present with my body and my emotions and grief especially. And then meditation became, I’m just gonna be present and sit with what’s here and feel deeply into my body, and there’s just so much that opens up. I’ve never heard of Untethered Soul, so I’m always interested in new book recommendations too.

Steven: Yeah, it’s very practical.

Why is anything here

Steven: So I begin to come at meditation from, I wanna understand how I work, how consciousness works. This begins to become a clear mystery. I learn about the hard problem of consciousness. It can’t be explained easily by material things. There’s just a few sticking points that just don’t make sense. And the question of why the hell is anything here? Why does this exist at all? That never left me since childhood. I will say I held onto a few big questions, and I never bought any popular lines for these things like, “I don’t know, man. Time is weird.” Existence is weird. Why am I here? Why am I feeling anything? This doesn’t make any sense.

And so I start to try to answer those. Where are thoughts coming from? How does my body work? How does it move? Where do my emotions come from? What is my sense of self? Why does it seem to stop at my skin, or does it? And you begin to poke all these things, and you go, “Holy crap, it does not.” And I can see where all these things are coming from, and they’re coming from not me. What is this? And you can peel these pieces back, and I begin to have insights that, oh, there is something here.

And so I dive even deeper, and I start taking it apart, right? This is still very left-brained, which is hilarious, but this is what draws me in. And then I read the Yoga Sutras. I’m like, “I’ll just go to the source of this. Here we go. Let’s see what these guys had to say.” And it’s great. And I begin to start to change the way I set goals.

Now, all of this does eventually turn into ghosts, aliens, and psychic powers, but it starts with self-improvement, and actually, that never stops. This whole path is self-improvement. I think I’ve mentioned to you that the most uncanny thing is that spiritual improvement and self-improvement, the ability to do things that do not make sense or comport with standard material physics, is directly in line with and really the same thing as personal development. Working through your problems is what gives you psychic powers. It’s the most hilarious thing in the world. I could never have predicted that. But we’ll touch on that.

I start becoming interested in unconditional states. How can I be unconditionally okay or happy? Or what is unconditional love? Can I extend that to other people? And I start thinking more in terms of non-duality of self. I am not just me, but I’m also sort of connected to other things. I can feel it in meditation. I feel these things start to happen. I start to think about non-polarities, like good versus evil is not really a real thing. I start to see the world differently. They are real, but they’re like forces, or they’re just like characters on a stage. It feels like not ultimately true. I see everything in shades of gray now. It’s more nuanced.

And then weird things start to happen to me. You wouldn’t think that, oh, meditating a lot and trying to learn these things, being nice to people, realizing that you are connected to other people… At this time, I still consider myself an atheist, a materialist. Nothing has really changed, but everything has started to change, right? At the same time, I’m living these things, not just in meditation, ’cause it’s making my life better. I’m happier. It’s easy to be happy.

And I start to change how I set goals, more from, how do I get a thing in five years to, it being more important how I feel right now and how am I carrying myself? Am I living my values right now? And more present-focused. It seems so token to say those sort of things. It’s very token advice. But I really leaned into it obsessively, because it made my life better. And the more I did, the more things I did not expect to start happening to me started happening. So I guess that would be the moment of rupture if I had to pick it.

Long lead-up, but I felt it was important ’cause it wasn’t a magical thing. Didn’t light a bunch of candles. I didn’t read any spells. I didn’t get into any of that stuff. I didn’t see any of this coming. I was just trying to be happier and understand myself and the world. That was it.

First-hand exploration

Joshua: The question about where do thoughts begin, I resonate with, and I’m curious about your experience being a fiction writer for such a long time. I read this book called Big Magic by Elizabeth Gilbert, and that was a paradigm-shifting book for me because she talks about stories as these beings that exist outside your own mind. And I think when I first began writing, I thought all my ideas came from my own head and maybe my life history and my interests. But the more I sat with it, the more I became comfortable sitting in the uncertainty and opening to what wanted to come through me.

My idea of where the stories come from totally shifted, where now I think that the story is a being that exists outside space-time or in another realm, another frequency of consciousness, I guess, and that I’m kind of this conduit, and it is expressing itself into the world through me. I’ll have no idea what I’m gonna write, or I’ll just have an inkling of it, and I’ll just sit down with my notebook, pen in hand, and I’ll just sit there in a kind of receptive state and then an idea strikes me and an image comes to me. And it’s not like I thought of an image. It comes to me.

And then the more I thought about that and practicing meditation, you’re really exploring your thoughts, exploring your consciousness. I’m also like, I don’t know, where are these words coming from right now? How am I forming words? How am I talking to you? How are we holding the thread of this conversation? All of that is just a mystery. It’s pretty weird.

Steven: Yeah. Nothing but mysteries. Even though I accepted the value, I had those models for materialism and the belief in reason and logic and scientific method, and all of these tools and models for understanding the world, they were very foundational, but they were not the most foundational beliefs I had. Those were really an accountability to truth and curiosity, and ultimately, those won out. It was not easy, but I pursued a first-hand exploration, right? That is another way of truth.

You can prove something through mathematics, or you can come near to proving a theory with science, right? To a degree of sufficiency anyway, right? Those are excellent tools. They’re still awesome. They’re still great. However, first-hand exploration is also just as valid, right? If you hear a crazy idea, and I’ve heard many, I just started following up on them. Like, “I’ll try it. We’ll see what happens.” I kept doing that, and it kept surprising me, and it kept working, and I haven’t stopped.

The first sighting

Steven: Not everything worked, but enough of them did that I began to realize, well, I guess we’ll go with the moment of rupture here. That would probably be the first UFO experience that I have, a sighting. I think at that point in time, the congressional hearings with David Grusch had happened recently, and I’m like, “What? All right, what is this?” I looked into this, and I’m like, “Did he just say what I thought he said?” And so I start looking into this, and then, of course, then I see one. I can describe the experience, but this one is not spectacular.

I’m just looking out the back window, and I see almost like a swarm of jellyfish. There’s just these flashing white lights in the distance on the horizon, and they’re firing like a neural network. It’s like do, do, do, do, do. Like in a series. And it’s just like a cloud. They weren’t drones. The light was weird. It was different. It was so bright, it was almost angry. It was piercing.

It was kinda like the sunlight is much brighter than a flashlight, but you can’t tell because our eyes have a logarithmic curve to them in terms of sensitivity. So a sunlight and flashlight might look similar, but the sun is way, way brighter than you think it is. This was like that. It was exceedingly bright, but only for a second. And I’m like, “Am I really seeing this?” That’s crazy. And that’s it. They just float by, and that started, of course… UFOs are a gateway drug, as many people know. Not by accident, as I later find out, of course.

And become obsessed about this. I needed to know because this doesn’t fit my world model. And I continue to meditate, I continue to explore, but now I’m starting to look into other things as well. And they start to come across my path. So those are extrasensory abilities. Even traditional stuff. Dean Radin’s books, get into those. Great stuff. These are modern takes on magic. The Yoga Sutras even describe siddhis. I start looking into that. I’m like, “What is this?” All these people are talking about this.

Maybe there’s something… I was wrong about this. What else am I wrong about, right? That is the one crack that shatters everything else, is, “Okay, well, I was really wrong. Why would I not be really wrong about all this other stuff?” So now I have to go do another, on my own. Fine. All right. Let’s go look into cryptozoology. Look into all of these things because I don’t know what else is wrong. And to my great surprise, many of them end up being true, so I start testing them firsthand.

I know what I saw

Steven: And I have another UFO experience. I’m in a pretty deep non-dual meditative state after a child’s birthday party. Only somebody with very young children could possibly understand why that would be necessary. It’s like a four-hour party, and by the end, it’s at an arcade. I’m just like, “I’m either gonna die or I’m gonna have to enter some sort of trance state here to survive.” So I do, and I’m pretty deep in it. I don’t have complete ego dissolution, but I’m a field of awareness.

We get home. I decide to go for a run, and I’m running, and I’m just watching a person run. So, cool. I’m pretty out there. And then I look up. I have a feeling I’m going to see something on the way home. I’m in the passenger seat. My wife is driving. I get home, and I go for a run, and I look up over my house, and I see a four-foot-ish tall, matte black kidney bean-shaped object floating in a straight line about a quarter of a mile behind my house, and it goes behind a tree line. It’s just floating in a straight line.

And of course, I see it. I’m pretty deep in. And I go, “Oh, yeah, that makes sense.” That’s literally the thought that comes to my mind after having almost no thoughts for a while. I’m like, “Yeah, that makes sense.” And then I start to get pulled out of that real quick, and I go, “Wait a minute. What the hell? No, no, no. What am I looking at?” That actually shouldn’t exist, and I snap back to association pretty quickly, and then I start to panic.

I wanna go jump some fences and go run at this thing ’cause it’s in a cul-de-sac in the neighborhood behind us. I’m like, “I wanna go look at this.” It’s got this mother-of-pearl sheen on it, and it’s floating in a line. This thing is not moving. And then I start to get very scared. I’m like, “Oh, I can’t do that.” If I go run and try to find this thing, nobody’s gonna know if I disappear. Literally nobody on this planet could help me if this goes wrong.

So that one really rocks my world because I know what I’ve seen, right? This isn’t just some lights in the distance. This is a physical appearing thing, right? And I know what I saw. I drew pictures immediately afterward. I tried to describe everything I could in my own journals, and that rocked my world, and I took a lot of these things much more seriously.

And then, of course, after that, I do end up having two more UFO experiences, but that is when I start to have more personal and profound experiences that are not in the sky or not happening outside of me, but are definitely happening to me. It feels much more personal and directed, so we can get into that, but whatever you wanna get into.

Joshua: Thank you for sharing that. I love the impulse that you had to run at the being, but then you’re like, “Maybe I shouldn’t.”

Steven: Actually, that’s a bad idea.

Joshua: I don’t know where this thing could take me or what this thing is.

Steven: Yeah. Who’s gonna save me?

Joshua: I feel like I’m a similar kind of thing. If I saw something, I’d be like, “Yes! Finally.”

Steven: You think you’d be excited, but when you’re actually there and it’s actually in front of you, you’re like, “Holy shit.”

Joshua: Ontological shock, terror.

Steven: I’m legitimately horrified. But I was also fascinated at the same time. I was really being ripped apart there.

You have to be shown

Joshua: I like the phrase you used, UFOs were a gateway drug. You had an experience that shattered your reality. But I think it happened because you were already doing all this inner work and meditation, and you’re already on this process of experiencing paradigm shift. And it kind of was the experience that could invite you into something deeper maybe.

My own experience, encountering the chakras in my body, I had what I understand to be a kundalini awakening, and really big energy body experiences, in many psychedelic journeys, and outside of them too. Similar to you, there were all these ruptures along the way, but that was a really big paradigm shift. I knew about them. I’d studied meditation. I was fascinated with the siddhis when I was an undergrad and beginning to study Buddhism, loving fantasy novels. I was like, “Oh, yogic superpowers. I’d love to have some of those.”

It was finally an experience that happened to me that was outside of the paradigm I had been inhabiting, and it was just really personal and intimate and real. These things exist on our peripheries. Everybody knows that people tell stories about UFOs or that there are people out there who claim to have had experiences with UFOs. Steven: You have to. You have to experience it firsthand. There’s no replacement. You can’t be told, and that is also a running theme here, is that you have to be shown. Being told is enough to get you to go, but you have to seek it out. This is so clear over and over again. You have to seek this out with your own curiosity, your own desire to do it firsthand. You have to struggle through it. You have to get shattered over and over again, and put your world back together, and you have to keep coming back for more, and then this whole world opens up to you.

And yeah, you don’t have to start with UFOs. It’s really a curiosity and a drive and openness to eventually accept what you have experienced and to make meaning of it, and then it will keep happening. That’s what happened to me.

So you described a sort of chakra experience. Wasn’t steeped in this world. I didn’t know what that was. I started to have experiences like that late 2024. I was doing a meditation. It was a long one. It went about two hours, and I was seeing visuals. It looked like music visuals, visualizations you might expect, looking through tunnels of various geometric colored shapes. I was seeing it in my visual field with my eyes closed. It was there. It wasn’t super bright, but I could see it. There was all sorts of symbols.

And then I felt a burning sensation in my chest, like it was a bonfire in there. And when I was done, it was still going, and then I was starting to get worried. After, I was feeling better than I’d ever felt before. But at the same time, I was like, “Why is there literal fire and heat in my chest? What is happening?” And so I had to go learn about these things afterward.

But for me, that was also very validating because it’s not like I imagined it. I didn’t know what this was. I had no idea what I was experiencing. It was just happening to me. I just stumbled into it. And so I had to make sense of events like that. Like, “Oh, okay. I guess this energy stuff is a thing. I always thought it was made up and stupid.” But I am absolutely feeling this and various other things. This is definitely happening. Somatically, I can feel it. This isn’t just imagination. This is bordering on pain, it’s so strong. Okay, something’s going on. I gotta go figure this out, which of course leads to the next domino, right?

Thoughts not my own

Steven: And these events just keep happening. I’m waking up in the middle of the night. I’m having profoundly vivid and strange dreams. I’m waking up in the middle of conversations. I didn’t get the first part of the conversation, but I see myself talking. Or I’m waking up in a liminal state, and my thoughts are going. I’m well aware my thoughts are not always my own. Some are more mine, less mine. It’s like a spectrum. And I’m talking. I’m watching myself talk, and I’m slowly waking up. I’m like, “Who the hell am I talking to? What is happening? Who am I talking to?”

These things keep happening. I have nightmares that feel extremely vivid, and there’s something there. I feel this instinctive terror, sense that, “Oh, no, this is happening.” I’m in a dream, but I’m not alone in my dream. There’s somebody or something else in my dream. Or I would wake up, and I would feel the sense of a presence. Or deep in meditation, I started to get thoughts that did not feel like my own. And I can say that because when you’ve been meditating for 45 minutes and you haven’t had a thought in 20 minutes, and it is just still as the ocean, you’re just like, “Yeah.”

And then you get a thought like, “Why do you come in here?” That was one of the ones I wrote down. It’s from one of the first verbal thoughts I got. I was like, “Why do you come in here?” ‘Cause I meditate in a closet. And it was loud. That is jarring. You’re not trying to make anything happen. There’s nothing on your mind. You’re just still as the ocean, but just like, “Hey, why do you come in here?” What’s going on? Where did that come from?

Joshua: Are you hearing this audibly?

Steven: That one was a verbal thought. I do get clairaudience sometimes, which is a sense of sound. It doesn’t usually have a source, but it is heard in the same way that you hear sounds. It just sounds like it’s coming from everywhere. If you were to play a sound through headphones, and it’s the same in both speakers, it’s kinda like that. I do get those sometimes. They’re not as common. It’s mostly verbal thoughts. But it was the out of context nature of the thought that was like, I absolutely did not make that happen. Wherever it comes from, however you wanna claim the cognitive science of it, this was directed. It was inserted into my head, and I start to get those.

At first, during meditation, and usually it’s helpful. I went through my old journals, and I start to go through these. What are they saying? It’s like advice. “You cannot give to others what you deny yourself,” right? Just hear that thought. I’m staring at a fire. I’m super dissociated. Then I hear, “You are the fire. You’re the same thing.” Just comes out of nowhere. So that starts to happen.

And then I start to get out of context emotions. As in, I’m feeling an emotion that is completely dissimilar to what I’m doing. I’m just chilling, just walking around, doing chores, and I feel a profound sadness or longing or curiosity, and the impression of a smile or that somebody’s watching me. It’s like, “What the hell is happening to me? Where is this coming from?” There’s somebody there. There’s nobody physically present, right? And so that starts to happen more and more.

And I have to start taking it seriously because, I mean, it’s scary. I don’t know what else to put that. It’s frightening, because I have no model for this. I don’t come from any sort of background. None of my friends talk about this, none of my family. This is not normal. I’ve got some pretty watered down, modern Christian parents. But for the most part, they don’t even go to church. I have no grounding. I have no idea what’s happening to me. And that’s the sort of stage I’m in.

I don’t know, starting a year or so ago, I guess, is when this starts to happen. But I’m pushing it away, right? This is not good. Everyone talks to themselves. This is not how talking to myself feels. And I already have a pretty fractured and chaotic mind on a regular basis, but this is new. Right? This is not how I normally experience things.

I ruled everything out

Joshua: What comes to mind is how our culture would make sense of those experiences would probably say schizophrenia, right? Hearing voices in your head, seeing things that aren’t there, things like that, versus there’s another way of understanding it as spiritual emergence, the shattering of a paradigm emerging into a greater reality, a broader universe where weird shit happens. And I’m curious, how do you remain grounded as all of these anomalous things begin happening to you? How do you not lose yourself in them?

Steven: I’d struggled profoundly with a chaotic mind and lack of self-control, emotional instability my entire life. I had learned how, frankly, to not trust myself but still rely on myself. I’d learned that I couldn’t, right? I already had that humility. I already knew what it was like to fall apart over and over again and put yourself back together because you gotta go to work next week, right? It doesn’t matter that you have literally zero motivation for no reason. It doesn’t matter that you’re incredibly depressed with no apparent cause. You gotta get it together, right? You just gotta get it together.

I had already learned how to control myself and a level of humility, put it that way. And those tools really came in, and I’d been meditating at this point for years, so I knew how to take an observer perspective. I knew how to step back and to be okay. So I had these things to fall back on, and I really needed every single one of those tools. And ultimately, I wanted to know what was true.

And although I’ve mentioned some scary things, most of the experiences, even now and especially now, more so, were very loving and strange, but not bad, right? And so it’s not like it was getting worse, like a haunting movie, you know? Where things just continue to get worse. It was getting magnified, but it wasn’t bad. And so that helped a lot as well. And I wanted to know. Ultimately, I couldn’t rest because I needed to know.

And of course, I ruled everything out. I seek psychiatric help. I obsessively research on my own. It doesn’t fit any of these models. I go through it. I’m reading books. I’m doing everything. And I’m being honest. At this point, I’m like, “I would love for this to be insanity. That would be great.” That would be way easier. Give me the label, so I’m all for it. But none of them fit, and no one would diagnose me with that because it didn’t fit.

It’s like, “You’re good. Dude, just don’t worry about it. Pursue this.” A very enlightened psychiatrist is like, “The worst thing you could do is suppress this. You need to go see this through and check it out. And as long as you’re not hurting anyone or you don’t feel like you’re in any danger or you can function, and it feels good and not bad, go. Go explore this. You need to find out who you are and what’s happening to you. I’m not here to tell you what’s not true. I’m here to tell you that you’re not crazy in the ways that we have learned to call DSM-5 labels. You don’t have any of these, or to the degree you do, you’re not dysfunctional. It’s not that. I’m not here to tell you what’s true. You go figure that out on your own.” Oh, cool. Pretty good psychiatrist. So I do.

The psi wheel

Steven: And I start learning other skills, like telekinesis. You can move things around, and that profoundly changes your whole world when you learn how to do that. It took me a long time. I stared at a piece of tinfoil sitting on a needle, commonly called a psi wheel, and it has very little friction, so it spins very easily. It’s just a little tent of aluminum foil over a needle stuck into a sponge or eraser. It’s got very little friction. That thing will turn easily. Basically a pinwheel. And you can use that to train, right? The goal is to move. Just however you do it.

I stared at that thing for 30 hours. I read a book on telekinesis. I was on board for an idealistic universe at this point. I mean, what else could explain all this crazy stuff?

Joshua: Could you pause just for a second and explain what idealistic universe means?

Steven: Sure. That matter is not fundamental. It is ideas and non-physical concepts, energy, consciousness is fundamental. It is consciousness and the things in its contents. Doesn’t mean there’s not rules, but that nothing is off-limits, basically. It’s imagination, right? There is no real line between imagination and material reality. It’s mostly a matter of patience and preference. But eventually, those two always converge. It’s more like a spectrum. So you have to take my word for it for now, but that’s my view of reality.

But yeah, after about 30 hours, I got that thing to move without touching it, and I recorded it, and I cried because it was so validating, right? You can have these crazy experiences, but it wasn’t you doing it, right? It felt like things that were happening to me. This was the first time that, “Oh, I can use this.” This is not only real, but I can do something. I’m not just a helpless victim. I can participate. I can do stuff.

Seeing without eyes

Steven: So I got better at that, and I start to get into remote viewing, and I start to use that as a way of exploring reality as well. And I’ve been practicing that almost daily for more than a year now. And then, as you well know, I learned other skills. I start learning about these things like, “Well, that’s probably true, so let’s go find out.” Sure enough, it is. Cool. So I go learn mindsight, the ability to see without eyes. I can do that. The viewers will have to take my word for it. I don’t mind demoing it if you want me to, but that’s not really the point. You can do this stuff.

And I began to take very seriously, it’s all just intention and energy and consciousness. Yes, there are rules, and there’s a lot of us on this rock, and of course, there are structures and patterns and friction. There are sort of metaphysical rules, but all of them are soft rules. Nothing is fundamental. It’s all just, like, has inertia to it. But ultimately, if you will hard enough, you can change the rules. Otherwise, senses like mindsight don’t make sense. You can’t see without eyeballs. It doesn’t make sense. I can close my eyes, put on a blindfold, and see like I have eyes. And once you can do that, your whole world changes.

As I do these things, it’s like stairsteps. The experiences that are coming back to me, it’s like I have a doorbell and a mailbox now and a front door, and I have a cell phone for the first time into the spiritual world, and they’re like, “Hey, what’s up? Hey, you can talk now. You can see things. You can touch things. You can interact. What’s up, man? You wanna talk?” And that starts to scare the hell out of me. I guess that’s really sort of the next leg of the journey, is not only can I do things, and I kind of understand some of how reality works, now it’s reaching back to me. “Hey, what’s up? Looks like you kinda see what’s going on now. You wanna talk?” And that’s more or less the stage I’m in now.

Looking at the sun

Joshua: I like when you were telling our group about zooming in on the sun. You wanna share that story?

Steven: Yeah. So that one gets weirder, but I’ll leave that part out. But I was just sitting there. Just having fun. A lot of this is play for me now because, yeah, it’s cool. I’m like, “I wonder what water looks like now if I just close my eyes.” ‘Cause the cameras sort of come out from where my eyes are. But I see through my eyelids as if they’re not there. And I, like, “Oh, I’ll just pour water on my face.” And I was laughing ’cause it was so cool. Normally, water isn’t in focus ’cause it’s on your eyes. And I could see it. It’s like you’re pouring water on the other side of a piece of glass. That’s what it looks like. And I was laughing. I thought it was hilarious.

And I’m like, “What else can I do?” So I go to sit down and read a book, and I’m like, “Oh, I’ll read this book without my eyeballs.” So I start doing it, and I’m like, the sun is in my eyes. My fake eyes. It’s still bright by contrast. It’s funny how much carries over. You’re still using parts of your brain’s visual system, of course. I don’t know how it gets there. I don’t know. Don’t ask me. It just does. It’s too bright by contrast. It’s really hard to read the book. I’m like, “What is this?” Coming in through the window on my couch. And I’m like, “But you know what? Joshua: With your eyes closed.

Steven: Or those special glasses or whatever, right? But I’m like, “Holy crap, I’m looking at the sun. Look, I can see the little tiny sunspots on it.” This is so cool.
