Here is my understanding of the general process and the sort of stages that you might go through. It’s, again, just based on sample size of one. And you can follow it if it feels right. Use your own intuition to know whether many of these things are useful.

So again, the goal is to get to a point where you have the direct realization that you are your reality, and you can feel that the stuff around you is you. And so there’s a lot of steps in between there, and they’re all very motivating and exciting, and honestly, they’re probably gonna be unique to you. I do think there are general patterns, so I am portraying this as if this is how it worked for everyone. I don’t know that. I think most of these things will probably be true for you, but your thing is gonna look like your thing.

I have a strong suspicion that your own personal journey here towards some transcendence is going to look unique and personal. This is all incredibly personal. It is remarkably personal. So I think it will be beautiful and wonderful and exciting and sometimes weird in exactly the ways you need, in an uncanny way. And the more you play along, and the more you take it seriously, the more the universe itself seems to play along and take your attempt seriously back. You can think of this all as like a challenge or a series of tests and a series of lessons.

I recommend holding on to these general ideas. If you can adopt these beliefs, it will help you. Everything means something. There are no small moments and small things. Begin to see meaning in every single thing that happens, small or large. “Why did that happen?” Begin asking those questions. “Why did that happen? How is that for me? What is that teaching me? What is the lesson in this?”

And the more you can apply that, and the more you can expect to see magic in the world… Whatever magic means to you. I’m mostly talking about synchronicity, which are meaningful coincidences. That is, “Wow, what are the chances that this thing happened? Oh, you were just talking about this thing, and wow, look, it just happened.” When you see those moments, encourage them and decide what they mean to you. “Is this magical? Was that for me? Is that saying something about my life in a small or big way that is relevant to me right now or will be soon? Is it just fun?”

And when you see them, notice them, acknowledge them. Feel free to say thank you, and take it seriously. And you decide what it means to you. You can’t be wrong. So when you notice those moments of magic in the world, and you expect to see it everywhere you go, everything means something. You’re training your own mind to look for it. But also, I think that there is definitely a reciprocating effect and that the world around you, your reality, begins to play back with you like a game of tennis back and forth. You’re playing now. And it’s fun. And it’s also helping you figure out what to do and what’s important, and it really gives you confidence that it’s working.

The rough progression

All right, so what are the rough stages of spiritual development towards transcendence as I understand it from where I am? What does it feel like? What are the effects? How do you know roughly where you might be on it? Everyone’s gonna be different, and it’s not like it’s one thing. It’s not a single scale. I’m reducing it to one scale here, but it’s not that. It’s like a thousand different skills and personal problems, and they all contribute. So we’re measuring an overall level, but in reality, you’re probably really good at some things already and probably pretty deficient at others. And you don’t have to be perfect. This is a game of iterative improvement.

And another excellent lesson before I give you the scale is that the problem you’re dealing with in life is always the right problem for you right now. It’s trying to teach you something, and that’s why you’re dealing with it. It’s a very useful mindset. “Why am I struggling with this right now?” Instead of saying it sucks or I have to fix it in a normal way, stop and ask yourself, “Why is this happening to me? What is this trying to teach me?” And assume that, “No, this is the right thing for me right now. I’m here for a reason. My job is to figure out why, to learn the lesson so that I can move on to the next one.” Take that viewpoint as well.

So here’s the rough progression. So at the bottom of the scale, you’ve got yourself now roughly, wherever you wanna put yourself on the scale, I guess, is that you begin to feel better, have more self-control, and become more self-aware. Your internal state, how your behavior affects others. You feel better. “I’m happier. Things don’t bother me as much. I’m less reactive, and I notice myself more, and I see myself more clearly. I don’t get caught up in my own dramas as much.” You’re on the scale. Congrats. There is a level of self-confidence that happens.

So as I mentioned before, personal development and spiritual development as it relates to having all sorts of wild encounters and effects and communication and insights and experiences, those come as the flip side of the coin of personal development. It’s so weird, but that’s how it works. So by improving yourself, you can talk to people with your mind and see without your eyeballs and move stuff. And you can know things before you should know them, all sorts of stuff. Your intuition tells you stuff about people. Or about situations that you didn’t expect. You have dreams that are teaching you lessons. All these things start happening, and it comes from improving yourself.

So as you’re moving up this scale, you will tend to care more and feel more and notice more as you go up. Higher you go up, I care more. I feel more of everything, by the way, not just good emotions, air quotes, “good emotions,” but everything. You can be brought to tears by staring at a bowl on a desk. You can also be brought to tears with sadness as you see somebody who is struggling or in pain, and you’re gonna feel that too. Your empathy is gonna go way up. So just remember, your aperture for feeling is gonna go wider as well. So you gotta be able to handle that.

You’re gonna be more sensitive overall, and you’re gonna notice more things. Your awareness is going to increase. And as that happens, you’re also going to care more and more in wider circles. So you can think of it as concentric circles. So yourself, this is why you need to start this from a point of self-actualization. “I know who I am, generally speaking. I feel self-confident. I feel healthy.” You can be unfulfilled. You can feel like, “Man, I’m not doing what I want. I feel like I have something I could be doing. There’s more. My life is good on paper. I’m pretty happy, or at least generally I am.” That’s a good starting point.

And if you’re not there, then there’s tons of self-development help, meditating and all that stuff. Well, trusting your intuition, that still applies. But anyway, you care about yourself, and you will care more about yourself, and then you’ll care about your friends, your family, your community. You care about things beyond yourself as you go up this scale, and you care more and more deeply as well. So it’s like you care more widely, but you also care more deeply. And that goes for feeling and noticing as well. You become aware of things in a way you weren’t before.

Extra sensory perceptions

Now, that awareness keeps going, and eventually you start to experience, and everyone has experienced these things, even if you don’t realize it. But your average level of awareness goes up, and you start to gain extra sensory perceptions. Again, often through dreams or when you’re waking up or going to sleep, those are the most common times. But now that you’re meditating, you’re gonna start having this happen deep in meditation. You’re just gonna have an insight on, “Oh, I know how to solve my problem.” Like, you didn’t even try. It just comes to you.

Or you will hear a thing, or you will feel a thing, and you don’t know why. Or you will have an idea, and then later someone will talk to you about that exact idea, and you’ll be like, “What the hell just happened? I was thinking about that this morning, and now you’re talking to me about it,” because somehow you knew before it would happen. Precognition. All those things are gonna start happening, and they’re gonna happen spontaneously at first. You will not be able to control it. And then if you practice with those skills specifically, you may bring them under your control. But they’re gonna start happening spontaneously.

Then you’re gonna get to a stage of non-physical communication. Your awareness will get to a point where somebody is gonna knock on your door. So be prepared for that. It will almost certainly be kind. But when you increase your aperture, you’re bringing in more of whatever you’re putting out there. So if you have a lot of fears or insecurities, you may attract those kinds of interactions. And if you’ve watched the other videos or listened to them, you have some idea of what to do about that. You have control over this.

But just know that you’re bringing your fears and insecurities with you, and to a degree, you are inviting those kinds of interactions. So if you’re incredibly fearful or you have very strong religious views that paint a world in which you are very likely to be attacked or you’re doing something wrong, you can expect some of these interactions to not be pleasant. So, you know, people are people, and creatures are creatures the world over. Just assume ’cause they’re not physical doesn’t mean that there’s not the whole gamut. But never forget that it’s your own mind, your own reality. And especially for non-physical things, you really just have to have a strength of self and to know, “You can’t actually hurt me,” and you’ll be fine.

And then eventually you begin to learn truths directly about the universe and the world. They just happen to you. You just know. “I know something profound about how reality works.” Or you start to get the answers to those big questions you started out with. You just know them one day. It just hits you or you experience it directly. And you know in a way that is not like, “I’m pretty sure I know how this works.” You’re like, “I just understand on a fundamental level how this works. I have a feeling I understand it.” And then you do. It’s pretty weird, and it’s pretty cool.

Anomalous events

So that’s generally how it works. But that sort of ignores what it’s like along the way and all the things that you could do. So that’s what it looks like to move along this sliding scale. All sorts of things will happen to you. You may have out-of-body experiences. In general, intention will make these things happen. You want them to happen, they are more likely to happen, but they can happen sporadically too. You may have vivid and lucid dreams where you’re interacting with people or something like people, and all sorts of things can happen. So I couldn’t tell you, but that’s the general idea.

So growth along this spectrum tends to happen in waves. You may have periods where you have an enormous amount of things happen to you all at once, and then nothing will happen for a while. So as long as you’re doing all that stuff, you’re seeking, you’re treating the problem of the day as, “No, this is serious. I’m struggling. This is happening to me for a reason,” and you are serious about trying to figure it out. No problem is too small. Why are you annoyed waiting in line? You tell me. Treat that as a serious problem. There are no small moments. Everything means something. There’s a lesson here.

If you’re playing that mini-game all the time, then you’re going to start experiencing things you did not expect. UFOs, someone will talk to you that isn’t there. You have a profound feeling that something is going to happen. The anomalous events are going to happen, and they are going to create basically a break in your worldview where you go, “Holy crap, this is actually happening. This is real. It’s working. I am aware of things that I did not know existed, experiences I did not know I could have. I’m pretty sure that this is happening right now.”

And when those happen, you have to process it. They can either happen in a way that you can tolerate. It’s within your change tolerance, even if it’s difficult. And I promise you, it can be hard. Some people will be harder than others. But you have to be able to process that.

So some things not to do when anomalous events occur to you. Flee, fight, denial, distraction, downplay it, or go catatonic. Now, I’m not judging anybody. If you need psychological defense, and it’s okay to run away or shut something down ’cause you can’t handle it anymore, that’s fine. But these things will not advance you, in the sense, if you always have that reaction, it’s going to stop happening. You’re shutting it down, closing that aperture, and it’s not gonna happen anymore.

So you want to ask questions like, “What happened? What is it showing me?” You need to decide what it means. Remember, you decide what it means. It’s up to you to decide what it means. Don’t let anyone else tell you what it means. This is a first-hand exploration. Remember, this isn’t about reading books. This isn’t about watching YouTube videos. I mean, this one aside, I guess, but this is just principles.

I’m not telling you what any of it is. I’m not telling you who these alien races are. I’m not gonna tell you who built the pyramids. I’m not gonna tell you that there’s secret cults. I’m not gonna tell you that magic, this is how it works. I don’t care. You go figure it out. I’m giving you the principles. You do it yourself. You decide what it means. Don’t abdicate that to anyone else. You can use other people’s information as a shorthand if you want, but in general, I’d say that is usually noise. Go find out yourself. You don’t need anyone else’s permission. You have everything you need already to do this. There’s no real reason to ask other people other than support. Support is great. Talk to other people who are doing something similar, these other kinds of inner explorations.

Act like it’s true

Now, after you decide what it means, you need to update your beliefs and your world models to try to incorporate this. “Okay, this is real,” so you don’t just project it. Like, “Okay, something is explaining this. I may not know what, but this happened for some reason, and can I fit this into my worldview?” And then you start to act from these new models.

So if you get a strong feeling that, again, I think I used this example before, that your deceased grandmother is trying to give you a message to do something, and you updated your belief, you processed that. “Okay, I’m not panicking too much. I got it together. Okay, this is possible. I’ve updated my beliefs a little bit.” Now you act like it’s true. You have to act like it’s true. Even if you’re not perfect, you have to act like it’s true, ’cause if you don’t want it, you’re gonna shut it down, and it won’t happen anymore. So you need to act it out. That means you don’t ignore your grandma, or whomever.

Whatever is happening, whatever you’re trying to do, whether you’re trying to move a piece of aluminum foil with your mind or see without your eyes, whatever you’re trying to do or hear or feel or understand, when it actually starts to happen, you have to integrate it, process it, and then start acting it out in the real world. So start acting like it’s true.

And if you’re in a safe space or you have a support group, talk it out. Tell them what happened. Bottling this stuff up, not good. Everybody needs somebody. And this is difficult. It can be difficult, and it can be isolating, and it could be ostracizing because it’s still not normal. It’s not normal to try to understand yourself by yourself using the tools that have been given to you from your very birth or before your birth. You have everything you need. You don’t have to ask permission, but that doesn’t mean that it can’t be isolating and lonely or disturbing to do these things on your own and feel like you can’t talk to anyone about it. That’s not good.

You need to be able to get this out in a safe space, so try to find people who are happy to share this kind of thing with you. That’s what you’re gonna do. Now, that’s if it went well and it’s within your change tolerance.

Shattered

Now, if you have a close encounter experience or you have a difficult dream or non-dreamlike experience or something happens to you that you’re not ready for, you thought you were, and then you’re like, “Oh, no, I am not prepared for this,” and you can feel yourself going into shock and shutting down… Obviously, once all this event is over, you have, I’ve dubbed the term, been shattered, basically. I’ve been shattered a number of times.

There’s a reason I suggested that you be able to handle your shit, because it’s one thing to say, “Oh, this is cool. Look at these photographs,” or, “Look at these ideas.” But when you see a ghost or whatever, a UFO or whatever, it actually happens to you because you’ve been trying to. Or when you see somebody do some healing or something, and you thought you were ready for it in an intellectual way, and then you see it, and you start to shut down and go, “Oh, no, that did not just happen.” Your worldview has been shattered beyond your ability to process and handle change. It’s not ideal for this to happen too much, but it probably is going to happen to you some if you’re serious about this.

And you need to be able to handle processing trauma, and that means I recommend go looking up how to process trauma. But the general idea is that how you encode the memory matters. If you encode this as something negative or, like, “This was a demon,” or, “I’ve done something awful,” or, “That happened because I’m terrible,” or, “I don’t know what I’m doing, I’m in danger,” you’re encoding that as something out of your control, and it’s a traumatic memory.

But if you can find a way that this was for you, that it was positive, that it meant something good even if it was confusing or disturbing… You need to find a way to process that trauma in a way that it doesn’t build up and prevent you from going. I mean, there’s no shame in calling it quits if it’s making your life worse. That’s not the point. Don’t be that reckless. But at the same time, you need to be able to handle traumatic events and know how to get through it all, with therapy, with friends, because it’s going to affect you. Generally in a beautiful way. It’s cool.

You go to sleep and then, oh, wow, your grandma, she’s there. She’s talking to you. That can either be something that shook your entire world and you shut down, and you don’t want this anymore, or, once you get through how difficult it’s been and you can reflect on it and process it a little bit, you can realize it’s something beautiful and awesome and exactly what you always wanted. How you handle it is what matters.

I will say these kinds of traumatic experiences, pain, change beyond your tolerance, and actually completely processing the events as they happen… Don’t just blow through them or downplay them and move on to the next thing. You really need to sit with this. You have to do a lot of thinking. At some point you’re like, “Oh, crap. The aliens are real, man.” Or whatever people call them, or spirits, whatever your thing is. I’m not here to tell you what’s true. Ghosts or, like, “I guess this is actually happening.”

The amount of times you’re gonna have to sit down and say those exact words, “Oh, this is actually happening to me.” And you’re gonna have to sit with that for a while and figure out what it means for you, and it’s not always easy. But you need to completely digest that. You need to completely work through it. You carry that through, the magic stops. Or worse, it doesn’t stop, and now you’re building up traumatic experiences, and that’s no bueno. That’s not gonna end well.

But in general, at least in my experience, the magic stops. The universe, your spirit guides, your deity, whatever, it stops the gravy train of cool and weird things happening to you because you’re not ready. If you’re not ready, it’s just pain. It’s just hurting you. You haven’t healed. You haven’t recovered. It’s like doing a workout where you injure your arm and you go back the next day and it’s like, “Come, give me more.” Like, no. You can’t. You’re not done with the last one yet. You hurt yourself, man. Or you just need to rest. It takes time. Irony of me saying it takes time. I was very impatient. I regret very little, but I learned these things because of my impatience, so I guess that’s to your benefit.

Things that do not work

Okay, so other things that do not work. Detachment from your human life and responsibility. Cool. At some point you’re like, “Pssh. You know, this is all a construct. I can control it all with my awareness,” or, “Why do I… I don’t need to…” Whatever your thing is. I don’t know what your thing is. But you could become disillusioned from your own regular life. “Why do I have to take the garbage out? I have had some deep insight and it feels so pointless. Like, what does it matter?” Don’t do that. It’s gonna happen, and that’s okay. Don’t judge yourself for it. But if you disconnect from your life, from your friends, from your responsibilities, the magic stops. Or worse, it doesn’t, and things get worse. But in general, it does seem to.

If you are acting with inauthenticity, or acting from a place of victimhood, it stops. No victims. It’s okay to feel like a victim for a while. It’s okay to go down, but you don’t stay down. So if you stay in those places, you’re faking it. Not in a fake it and you make it, like, “I’m living into something,” but like, “I’m acting like not myself, and I don’t like it.” If you’re not acting authentically in your life, doesn’t work.

This is, remember, personal development. That’s all we’re really talking about is personal development, and you just keep going off into the stratosphere. It’s escape velocity. You keep developing that person so hard that apparently you can leave your body, talk to aliens, and see ghosts. I don’t know, man. I don’t make the rules. It just happens.

Okay. Failure to retain or live out previous lessons. When you learn those lessons throughout life, “Why is this happening to me right now? Well, because I judge myself super harshly, especially when I fail or I feel rejected.” Great. If you manage to work through that behemoth of a problem that you’ve had, and then two months from now you regress and you go from being able to see without eyes really well or whatever your thing is, or you just feel like you’ve regressed spiritually or nothing’s happening anymore, you need to reexamine whether you have actually continually applied the things you’ve already learned.

If you’re not doing what you already know to be true, at least on a general basis… You can’t just learn it once and then forget about it. You need to keep applying those lessons. This all accumulates. It’s stacking up. So if you stop taking care of yourself, if you stop doing the basics or you’re repeating mistakes that you’ve done in the past over and over again, you’re not gonna progress. In fact, it literally all stops.

Irrational belief

So the general path of progress from where you are now to whatever you’re trying to accomplish next or answer some big question or just a general spiritual development is a process of acting from irrational belief. So you are reaching out, acting beyond what your existing models of the world can predict. So if you come from a materialist worldview, everything is atoms and physics, then it’s going to be hard for you to experience non-physical energy or chakras or whatever you’re trying to do.

So you need to be able to believe something. Like I said, you need to be able to sit with a stupid idea long enough to see if it’s true. So you’re holding it in this space where, “I believe it can be true. I believe it can be true.” It can be that little engine that could until it does, and that could be longer than you think.

A mirror or a flashlight

All right. What else did I not cover? Oh man, there’s a lot I’m not covering. Sheesh. This stuff is complicated, but it’s also not that complicated. There’s a general idea that you will be helped. There is somebody for everybody, and they are trying to help you. And if you sincerely embark on this, there are people who are putting lessons in front of you, just like I’ve described my own stories, like a curriculum. They’re always trying to push in front of you what you need now. What you might need now is to not yell at your kids. That’s okay.

It’s almost always a problem within yourself. It’s some insecurity. It’s something you haven’t faced. So if you don’t wanna face your daddy issues or you don’t wanna face your insecurities about your appearance or whatever, I got bad news for you because this whole thing is a machine designed to make you face every single one of your dark parts or things you don’t like about yourself or the world or your ignorance. It is literally just a mirror or a flashlight. You’re seeing yourself, and you are shining a light on the portions of yourself that you probably least want to look at.

So you don’t get magic powers unless you’re willing to face your childhood traumas. And that’s just how it is. Or your relationship issues, or your issues with yourself, because they are what is preventing you from getting to that moment, as strange as that sounds. So those are called distortions, and they are just things that are in the way. They’re illusions. They are things that you think are true that are limiting you, and they’re hurting you, and they’re making your life worse.

This is literally just a process of improving yourself. So if you think you don’t have to face something or there’s one fear you won’t have to get through… That doesn’t mean that you have to solve everything. But in general, you should expect each of these lessons and even the anomalous experiences to be shaped by, and you should know why by now, your own fears, insecurities, and limiting beliefs. That’s really the work you’re doing because, again, it’s all you.

If your reality is all you, the only thing preventing you from being aware of and controlling your reality is yourself, and these are the things that are in your way, is these distortions. It’s personal problems, largely speaking anyway. Not entirely, but largely. There is a level of just work that has to happen and increased awareness. There are skills that happen. Deliberately dumb

Some things to be aware of. Frustration, confusion, and absurdity are fundamental properties of these experiences, and you have to exert effort to overcome them. And the reason that this is this way is because it improves the potential. So if you see something and it’s a little insane, I don’t know, like Bigfoot or something… Some of this stuff is deliberately dumb. It doesn’t make sense. It almost precisely doesn’t make sense.

It’s almost precisely calibrated to happen in a way that it’s just outside or maybe way outside what you believed was possible, in a way that you definitely didn’t believe, or it triggers your own insecurities or is related to your own fears. It is the exact thing you didn’t want to see or experience. But not necessarily in a scary way, or there will be properties about it that don’t make sense that bother you. So it’s frustrating or it’s confusing.

And the reason it is that way is because you’re being shaken. Like, “Hey, pay attention. You can’t ignore this.” That’s the idea. The whole thing is designed to be something you can’t ignore, so you’ll take it seriously. So just know that that will happen, and these are common properties of any of these experiences, is it’s either dumb, it doesn’t make sense, in a way that is like, “This is impossible,” but also silly.

I went to sleep the other night, and as I was trying to fall asleep or taking a nap, I heard like a baby whining. Just one sound effect, and I’m like, “What is this?” This has no relevance in my life, and nothing baby-related has happened since then. It’s like, why? Just because. I don’t know. Who knows? Because it could. It’s almost like a, “Eh, bet you didn’t think this could happen.” The reason that happens is it sticks in your mind. It keeps you going. It keeps you questioning, like, “That wasn’t possible, right?” That is a fundamental property of these experiences. Don’t be surprised by that. You should be expecting it.

Set an intention

Anything else to cover? I think, again, support, super important. Intention is just directed, purposeful energy with attention. Your information diet

And so the final thing I’ll also say is just taking very seriously how you allow things to influence your life. You’re going to need to roll back. You’re just happy. You’re content with almost anything.

You are best getting rid of the… If something makes you feel bad, if you feel worse afterward than when you did it, there’s a very good chance that this is in the way of what you’re trying to do here, and it is limiting you because you believe you need it. That can be drugs. That can be entertainment media. It doesn’t have to be. There’s nothing wrong with those things inherently, but why you do it is what’s important. And that ain’t easy to say. You don’t like it? Well, okay. You want control over your reality. You want the gift of everything. Do you want to be able to do anything or be anything? I don’t… I assume that’s where this goes. That’s the message I got. Do you want that? You wanna live in your little world where it’s like this, and you believe you can’t do things and you need drugs or something else to unwind, and nothing else is okay? There’s no judgment on that.

You’re gonna have to get rid of self-judgment. You’re gonna have to learn how to stop beating yourself up and stop blaming yourself. You have to be kinder to yourself. If it’s not making you feel better, that should be very questionable. You should be happier and feel better all the time. You should feel more connected to your world, to your own life, to the people in it.

Leaning into your life

You lean in. This is all a process of leaning into your life. It’s not leaning away and trying to separate from it. If you do it seriously, you do it intently, you’re serious about removing things from your life and changing yourself in a positive, healthy way to feel better, to feel more connected to the world around you, to be more at peace, to be happier no matter what is happening to you, this stuff will just start happening, and it will keep happening as long as you continue doing the work that got you there. If you stop doing the work or you stop believing it, it will close, and it will stop happening, and you’ll stop growing.

Prepared to be weird

So the last thing I’ll say is that you need to be prepared to be weird. If that’s not already obvious, you should be prepared to feel different than the people around you. But of course, as this happens, you’re going to feel compassionate, and you’re gonna feel sorry for them. As you gain these insights, you’re going to feel out of place. You need to have a community somewhere online somehow. You need to talk to somebody. You need to not feel alone in this. Because it can be very isolating, and you may feel the desire to talk to people who aren’t ready for this.

It is my belief, and it is the beliefs of the people that I’ve talked to and had interactions with, and is evident in my own curriculum—in retrospect, I didn’t know for a long time that there was a curriculum, but you can see a path in retrospect of assisted growth—that you cannot push something onto somebody that they’re not ready for. If they don’t want this, first of all, it’s wrong to shatter their worldview.

I don’t walk up to people and show that I can see without my eyeballs because it could really harm them if they’re not ready for that, and it could really damage this whole enterprise. And socially speaking, if you really want people to turn against you and say, “This is wrong,” that would be a good way to do it, is to terrify them without giving them the context of, “No, seriously, you can do this,” like helping them through it.

But generally speaking, if somebody isn’t already interested, you’re gonna have to feel it out. You’re just gonna have to let them be. Don’t try to fix other people, and don’t try to blow the doors off and force them through it because it is wrong, and it doesn’t work, and it will cause them to shut down, and you’re just going to traumatize them because they don’t have the same tools to recover and make sense of what’s happening to them that you are developing. So you’re just hurting them. You’re hurting them, and you’re also hurting this whole enterprise. You’re making it harder for other people to talk about this because they’re gonna demonize it.

So if you don’t wanna do that, and you want it to be a beautiful thing to figure out who you are and explore the nature of your own reality and big questions and connect beyond yourself, then don’t make us look bad. And don’t scare people who aren’t ready for it.

Helping other people

But at the same time, one of the most important things you can do to supercharge this whole process, besides being yourself and doing what you feel is right, surprise, surprise, and improving yourself, is helping other people do the same or in any way. And of course, as you feel more and care more and notice more, you’re naturally gonna wanna help people. If you’re already following your intuition, you’re gonna want to start doing it because you care more and because you feel connected to them. And then eventually, you start to feel that, like, you’re the same person. You’re more than just connected. On some level, we are the same, and you want to help them. It just makes sense. It’s obvious.

Not only do you not wanna hurt them anymore, or you don’t care about negative stuff. You don’t even wanna entertain it anymore ’cause you can feel it affecting you. You want to help people that are in a bad place. So that is totally cool. Go do that every time you feel it. And you’re gonna have to learn how to do that, but, however excited you might be, don’t try to force this on other people. You’re either gonna traumatize them, or you’re gonna scare them off. You’re gonna give them things they’re not ready for. And the whole process is just enough.

This is like working out. You don’t wanna drop 1,000 pounds on top of somebody’s head. They’re not gonna get strong like that. They’re gonna get crippled, and they’re gonna get scared, and you’re gonna mess them up. So don’t do that. You don’t have to keep it to yourself, but just be aware of what you’re doing and how it affects others.

And with that, send me a message with your mind one day. Be polite. Maybe I’ll say hey if I know how to do it, if I get it. Good luck, and have fun. Just remember that this is a profound and personal experience. Don’t be greedy. Share it when you can, and be good. That’s the whole point because it’s all just you anyway, so you’re only hurting yourself otherwise. Have fun.

A short one, and the lightest. A dream about a cooking show turns into a lesson about food from someone who gives his name as Nom. He dates the experience to the morning of May 3, 2026.

It is also the last recording in the feed, released October 3, 2026. He tells listeners it is the very last episode, and his final words in it are “See you around.”

Hello. This is Steven Schrembeck, and I am recording the last bonus episode here. This one’s titled Food and Frequency. This is a lecture-like experience that I got on the morning of May 3rd, 2026. Before, during, or after

So he goes on to explain that a batch of Oreos could be made with love, and that you can tell the difference when something is made with love, and that there are three different ways to determine the frequency of a food as it relates to its health value for you. You could get to that level.

So then I say, praying over your food, therefore, is not completely useless. And their response, direct quote, “Not at all,” and a smile. Then I ask another question. “Does feeling guilty change the food’s health value?” Quote, “In a sense, but by a different mechanism.” And I got the sense that this was a different lesson, but it was related to this. And I quote, “Suffice to say, you are affecting yourself more than the food in this case. So you’re directly affecting your own body and its ability to digest fully this food, possibly among other changes.”

Keeping a promise

So at this point, I pause. I’d been going through this whole notes and making sure I understood the things that I’d said, and I got the quote, “You understand,” followed by a sort of confident feeling, a simple assertion. So it stopped sort of fussing over getting the exact words right. You get it. Time to move on. You get the idea. Let’s go.

And I said, “Thank you for the lesson,” and that I would pass it on. So here I am, keeping a promise. And then they said, “I know.” And then there’s a knowing and warm smile. “Thanks for your work.” And I said, “Same. Bye.”

And there’s one more interaction. I wrote down, “This conversation requires cookies,” and then I ate two of them. And Nom replies, “They were gluten-free. How do you feel?” And I wrote down the word, “Eh.” And there is a knowing smile, followed by the indication to, “Well, maybe you should pay attention to that.” And this was clearly a joke. I didn’t even realize they were still paying attention. “Yeah, you ate those cookies. How do you feel? Oh, they’re gluten-free. Hmm, and you don’t feel good. That’s interesting. Guess there’s more to it.” And that was it. That was the end of the entire interaction.

What I took from it

And I thought that was so fun. Those are some pretty weird ideas. I’d never heard those before. I definitely never thought about a food being healthy based on how its experience was. What was the quality of its own experience, and that it’s giving you that quality directly. It’s imparting that on you. So if it was grown with love and tender care and it was well cared for.

I mean, a lot of these things directly correlate with more obvious facts, like if a factory farmed animal was very sick and tortured and stressed out, chances are that food is not very healthy for you. Same with a whole bunch of food that has been processed or sat around stale. And I never considered that a lot of the reason it might be unhealthy is not because it was old or because of pesticides or because of runoff or whatever was happening, pollution, or because of contaminants, but because its own experience was bad and all the things it was connected to had a bad experience.

And if you follow that thread, it is interesting. It’s still saying the same thing. It includes a model of, well, you know, it was infected with this fungi, and so that’s not good for you. Well, that could still be true, but it was also not good for you because this grain didn’t have a great experience because it was infected with fungi. I thought that that was very fascinating, and it opens up the avenues of being able to affect the quality of your food and thus your health value in other ways.

I think everyone can attest that for some reason, the same meal made at a restaurant is often less satisfying and it feels less healthy than a home-cooked meal. You can make pretty crappy food that is made with love at home, and then served with care. And for some reason, that does seem to make a big difference, and I think that this model helps to explain why that might be.

I thought they were very cool ideas, and I’d never heard frequency described in this way before, and that it’s all contextual. And I thought that was fascinating too. So I hope that you found this interesting. Thank you all very much for listening. This is the very last surprise episode. See you around.

Part Three Earlier Conversations

Selections from four interviews Steven gave before the series, mostly to hosts who had asked him about technology.

Self-Control and Mental Clarity

A conversation with Dr. Christopher Loo

Jeff Cameron · before you read

Part Three steps back. These four interviews come from before the series, and in them he is a founder being asked about technology.

I put them in because the man in Part One did not come from nowhere. This first conversation has no anomalous experience in it at all. It is about what he called the untrustworthy operator: how you rely on yourself when you know you can’t. His answer is to notice what a thing does to you and then arrange your life accordingly, without waiting for willpower to show up. I think it is the same move he makes later, with much stranger material.

Editor’s context: Steven was a guest on the Financial Freedom Podcast, hosted by Dr. Christopher H. Loo, who introduced him as a startup founder from Georgia, just outside Atlanta, working on AI for self-control and stability. What follows is a selection from their conversation.

Tools that help people

Chris: You have a very interesting background. Talk about your early experiences and what you’re doing.

Steven: I’m gonna sound like the opposite of impressive at first because I started out in college thinking that computer programming was something that was too hard, and then ended up switching into a computer science related major. From there, I did pretty routine software development, and then I got a job at Amazon, not AI related, just regular software. About 2016, I started realizing that deep learning is having a second renaissance. This is incredible stuff. This is now entering the level of complexity where as a regular software developer you can begin to understand it with a little extra work.

So I started training deep learning models, mostly vision models, some audio models, stuff like that. And then of course there is a third wave of AI revolution here where natural language processing, the advances there combined with transformers and other deep learning models to give you large language models that you’re all familiar with. And all right, great, so now I’m gonna learn this. It’s at an even higher level of abstraction. And so I’ve been building my own stuff for years, some models as well through my day job, and now I’m applying everything I’ve learned to just make tools that help people. Not sensational AI, just really practical. I’m using it where it’s the right thing to do.

A hazmat suit

Chris: You talk about developing AI tools to help individuals regain control over their attention and choices in a increasingly distracted world. Elaborate on that and what that means.

Steven: A lot of these general concepts you can start to apply in your own life. You don’t need to use anything that I’m making or anything else. But think of it this way. Large language models specifically, and all the stuff that gets bolted on top of them, I’ll just shorthand it to large language models. There’s a lot of magic under the covers. It’s the same with Google. You type in a search box, but you just know that there’s an ocean of code under there. So there’s a lot of stuff that goes into this, but for now assume it’s large language models.

It’s basically commodity intelligence. You can think of it as not like an especially bright person, just like your average person, an average level of reasoning in a box. A general reasoning, and that is really powerful. One thing you can use that for, yes, you can use it to replace people. However you feel about that, good or bad, is neutral. But you can use it to replace commodity intelligence. Everyone tends to focus on that.

But you could also use it in places where it would be unethical, unreasonable, or just not preferable to have a human do the same thing. So think of it like a hazmat suit, or if you’ve seen the show Chernobyl or whatever, where they try to get the robots to go in and clean it up. So you put it in environments where you wouldn’t want a human. So you can imagine there’s a lot of environments like that on the internet. It’s a lot more dangerous, predatory. There’s a lot of influences.

So some simple ways to use it are just send an AI ahead of you just to go look for things that it’s triggering or is it good, and just pacify it or sanitize some of the things, or even just censor some of the things you don’t want to experience. Get rid of the noise, the intentional bias and influences, all that sort of stuff. So you can think of this as having a hazmat suit.

So that is a simplistic way of looking at that, but there’s a lot of other environments and situations in which you would like to have human-like intelligence evaluate a situation without a human so that a human doesn’t have to. So not just saving work, it’s also sparing a conscious creature from having to experience or go through this.

The untrustworthy operator

Chris: You talk about the AI tools assisting those struggling with ADHD or addiction. How is this possible? What are specific features designed to help those struggling with ADHD and addiction?

Steven: There’s lots of different hows. It’s like a constellation of different tools, and a lot of it’s just regular software. But one of the fundamental problems is you have the untrustworthy operator problem. Let’s say you’ve got a bunch of system that keeps you healthy, in check. “I meditate at this time. I take my medication at this time. I don’t go buy drugs or alcohol ’cause I’m not doing that. I make sure that I check into work, or I leave by this time.” You can imagine a whole bunch of systems that people with ADHD and autism rely on.

However, the operator themselves is not trustworthy, both in the sense that they forget, they have low object permanence, they just don’t even realize that they should be doing this, or they have emotional irregularity or motivational instability. So you simply can’t operate in a way that’s… Okay, “I have a personal rule not to go buy alcohol on the way home.” Okay, that’s not gonna stop you. The operator themselves can’t be trusted.

So now what do you do when the system you rely on, you can’t trust the person who’s operating it? And you can have a nanny, or you can have, I guess, a babysitter. You could have a sponsor. There are piecemeal solutions to all this stuff. But what would be really nice is if you had a system of accountability that understood the nuance. There’s that commodified intelligence. This is where the AI part comes in. “What state are you in? Can I trust you? Yes or no?”

Normally you can change your rules. It’s just a rules engine. “I do this, I don’t do this, make it hard to do this, hide this from me.” It knows you can’t be trusted, so there’s this balance. This is where you need the intelligence. “Do I let you just turn all this stuff off? Do I try to block a transaction at a liquor store?” “Okay, I’m just buying this in order to make a flambé later.” There’s a lot of nuance here that brute force, simplistic tools don’t work.

So you need something that understands. Also, you don’t want it interrupting you or stopping you or blocking your websites when you’re trying to do stuff for work. There’s an enormous amount of nuance in when to apply enforcement, and the net result of all this is that it just makes it easy to do what you wanna do, harder to do what you don’t wanna do, and it makes it more or less impossible to not realize what you should be doing. And then the rest is more or less just set up your own rules.

So that’s the general system. It’s for sleep, it’s for electronic addictions, avoiding substance abuse, spending control. These are the most common problems that people have, and so that’s what we’re focused on. But the principles are general. It’s whatever rules you got for life. But just imagine a little guardian angel that is constantly paying attention to what you’re doing. There’s some AI there as well. And then whatever information you choose to give it, it’s using that to evaluate whether it should step in to enforce your own rules or not if you don’t.

A digital adult in the room

Chris: How do the AI tools you’re developing help users attain mental clarity, and can you share examples of how these tools are used?

Steven: I’m operating from the state that your standard human at rest is already healthy, so if that’s not true, that’s a different target audience. So assuming that at rest when healthy you’re functional, then it is mostly about removing influences. It’s about decreasing your level of stimulation to improve your baseline dopamine. So a lot of this is like the hazmat suit I mentioned. It’s creating different interfaces to influences. Okay, you wanna listen to podcasts? You go through this other tool that desanitizes it. You wanna browse the web, you want to look at social media, you’re gonna do it through this alternate interface that doesn’t allow these things to pull your attention away.

You can also tell it what your intentions are. This is what the first version of the software was. Tell it what your intentions are, and then it’s paying attention to what you’re doing, and if there’s a discrepancy, it can just make you aware. You’re doing this to yourself. If you choose to follow up by adding more restrictions, “Hey, I said I was doing this thing for work and I’m not doing it after 45 minutes,” go through this pretty well-known psychotherapy routine, mirroring.

None of this is new. I’m just putting together existing Legos. “Follow this step, then this step. If I completely ignore it, then I want you to turn off my internet.” Not everyone’s gonna do this kind of thing, but for certain problems it makes sense. Like substance abuse, for instance. You need a hard guardrail which says, “If a transaction comes through or is trying to be processed from these merchants or in this way, deny it.” And that’s just a hard enforcement.

It’s never gonna be able to stop you. The purpose is just to add friction or to remove the presence of things that are making your life worse so that you don’t have to use willpower. The whole purpose is you cannot rely on willpower. These people specifically, I’m definitely in the target audience, cannot rely on willpower reliably.

So it adds a backstop, and ultimately allows you to retrain your brain for what you want and what you pay attention to because there is a digital adult in the room that will eventually step in, and that is something that we lose when neurodiverse people come from childhood to adulthood. There’s nobody to step in anymore if you live alone or you’re supporting yourself or others are dependent on you. You need reliable ways to not crash and burn. There’s no one to step in.

And so now this sort of preserves autonomy and independence and allows some software to step in by your own design. You’re doing this to yourself. It’s configurable. So that’s the premise.

People know what to do

Chris: What are your thoughts on society’s current challenges with self-control, and how can technology, particularly AI, foster more mindful and intentional behaviors?

Steven: Anyone relatively studied in psychology or sociology recognizes just how we like to think we have free will, and like we have a lot of agency. But most of our behaviors and the ways in which we can be influenced are very well documented. They’re super well-documented blueprints on how to get humans to do what you want them to do. And the sad part of this is, that has been very well perfected and engineered upon by basically everything. Everything from business processes to the well-known attention and influence economies, ads, all this sort of stuff. It’s obvious people have figured out really how to perfectly manipulate others through technology.

The nice part is that you can do that to yourself, and you can also detect that, and you can stop it. So that is the role of software in this, is that you can use software to harm yourself or to be harmed, and you can just accept that. Or at the individual level or an organizational level, you don’t have to wait for society to change. You can just make your own tools or set your own rules. None of this has to be software. But what to access and what not to access, and become aware of what’s manipulating you or influencing you.

And so that is really how I see it, is yes, collective action is great. The man is slow, frustrating, and often ineffective. Solutions are not great. So if you’re waiting for US Congress to pass laws banning social media so that your 12-year-old isn’t being unduly influenced, I think you’re gonna be waiting a long time and you’re gonna be disappointed.

However, you can begin to take action now. A lot of it’s simple, and a lot of it is the willingness to notice how things are making you feel. That is such a simple thing to say, but notice what is making you feel bad, become committed to that, and then be willing to live differently or to be creative on how you remove or blunt those influences on you. That’s the most important thing to do regardless of what tools you use, ’cause nothing is ever perfect. You gotta fit it to your life. Remove the bad stuff and do more of the good stuff.

I don’t think that any of this is actually surprising. My insight for building a business in software was that people know what to do. The average person knows what is healthy for them and not healthy for them generally, but they have trouble actually doing it or not doing it. So that’s where you should focus. Less on shame. Stop using effort and willpower, and just make it so that it’s really hard, if not impossible, to do the things that are bad for you, and it’s really easy, if not inevitable, to do the things that are good for you.

The cutting edge

Chris: AI can feel impersonal. How do you ensure your tools foster genuine human agency and are accessible? There’s this concern from people that have access to AI and that know how to use it. They’re gonna use it to, of course, enrich themselves and progress, whereas the common average person that doesn’t know is gonna get left behind. How do you address this?

Steven: That’s a profound problem, and I think that it’s accurate. But that’s a question that is well beyond the scope of just AI. This is any technology, what happens when you have exponential abilities. Some people are becoming superhuman. That’s a major challenge that is really a philosophical one. So I won’t weigh into that too much other than I would advise you don’t need to be on the cutting edge. But usually being about 20, 30% behind the cutting edge, being an early-ish mainstream adopter is about good. Just pay attention and try to use these things. There was a second part to that question that I forgot.

What are you replacing

Chris: With AI, how do you help people use technology to create more meaningful lives? Ultimately, with technology, we’re losing meaning. We’re very connected, but we’re some of the most disconnected people in human history, even though the technology is supposed to bring us together. So how do you use AI to create more meaning for people?

Steven: That’s a great question, and I’m willing to be late for my next thing to answer that because that’s an amazing question. And I feel very strongly about this. We should not be replacing the behaviors that we want to do. This is where capitalist profiteering, like efficiency mindset, falls flat. If we keep going, we’ve automated away everything. We have AI raising our children. We have technology that does everything for us. Eventually, it can just live for us, and what are we? You can just be put on a feeding tube and just go unconscious.

What are you replacing? I think this is where we have to be quite mindful. At an individual or even an organizational level, what are you omitting? What are you actually replacing? Did you get rid of the things that you wanted to do? The purpose of an AI tool is the obvious that most people talk about, to replace the jobs and tasks that people don’t want to do, or that it can do it better. Sure, that’s fine. Go use it for those things. That’s obvious.

Pay attention to where it’s overrunning and recognize that there is meaning in work, there’s meaning in helping people. What are you doing with this extra time? Unfortunately, if the common denominator, the status quo, is that you go back to the attention economy, you start grazing for information, or you’re in a world where nobody else is around, in the physical world it’s very lonely because they’re all zombies, that’s bad.

What are you freeing up your time and energy for? So you want time, energy, and attention freedom. That’s awesome. You can keep opening that up, but if the way you allocate it is just pointless leisure or it has no meaning for you, then you’re just getting more of a terrible thing.

So this is, again, a neutral tool. All it does is give you more time, energy, and attention freedom, maybe some financial freedom as well. But what are you gonna do with that? You have an excess, you have a surplus of these things, but how you allocate that for meaning is critical. It creates more of a thing, but it doesn’t mean that you’re gonna use your newfound freedom and resources. That is an entirely different conversation and problem. I have a lot to say on those, but I think we’ll have to leave it at that for now.

About helping people

Chris: What if people wanted to reach out to you and connect with you, how could they do that?

Steven: If you’re a software dev or a designer or project manager, I’m looking for collaborators. This is a lot of work. Now that I’ve started doing user interviews and all this stuff, running a business is crazy hard. I don’t have time for all this. The loose collection of contractors plus me is not cutting it anymore.

So yeah, if it sounded cool and you wanna try it out or you wanna help, this is mission driven. It’s got profit motive because this is America. You can’t do anything without a profit motive. But really, this is not the easiest way to make money. This is about helping people. So if that sounds cool, give it a shot.

The Technology That Centers You

A conversation with Jack Wagoner

Jeff Cameron · before you read

From June 2025. Almost nothing in it is strange and almost all of it is him: building tools for his own mind since he was a teenager, a thousand hours of meditation, the chair test, and his definition of happiness as how you feel when nothing is happening.

It is the nearest thing in this book to an ordinary conversation with him.

Editor’s context: Steven was a guest on The Grateful Podcast, hosted by Jack Wagoner, in an episode published June 11, 2025. Jack introduced him as the founder of Impossible Laboratories and the maker of Threads, a wearable first meant for people with ADHD. What follows is a selection from their conversation.

The impossible

Jack: Steven, welcome to the show.

Steven: Thanks, Jack. I didn’t write that intro, by the way. If I had done, it would have been way worse. It would have been way more blunt and humble.

Jack: I’m so fascinated by what you’re building. Impossible Laboratories. What’s impossible with what you’re chasing? Where did that name come from?

Steven: Well, you’ll pick this up over the course of our conversation, but I’m not big on pretending to make things more exciting than they are. I see most of the world this way. It’s pretty straightforward. That is a label, and yes, it’s a formal company and has a tax ID number and all that, and there’s people associated with it, but it’s just a label for the things that I make, because that’s what inspires me most. I needed a wrapper for all the projects that I’m doing.

I am motivated to prove the impossible possible. I’m motivated really to see what’s at the edge, what can be done that hasn’t been done before. I’m fine with proving it impossible. That’s just as exciting. But it’s about curiosity, a specific kind of curiosity. It’s where the name comes from.

Jack: And so that edge you’re trying to go over, is it technology aiding us in becoming more present? What is that hump?

Steven: To some degree, I leave that up to the people who want to use these things and make it their own. I try to separate my beliefs and prescriptions for what I think is ideal. What is the right level of integration? What’s the right way to use technology? I try to stay away from that. I mean, I can’t keep myself out of it, for when you make something, you’re always making a statement one way or another. So I try to separate those things.

My personal belief is a dramatically different way of using technology, not necessarily less, and that this is sort of a distraction, to think that less is what’s necessarily better, but the quality of two different things. How I measure my life in terms of happiness and fulfillment is the experienced self and remembered self, sort of popular psychological framing. So that is, how do you think about yourself when you reflect on your life and your memories? And then how does it feel to be you in the moment? And that those are both important, and as long as those are both doing well, then that’s fine, whether that’s appropriate use of technology or anything else.

Since I was a teenager

Jack: So what made you want to pursue this merging tech and human experience? A lot of people have attempted and greatly failed at this. I think social media was intended to do a similar thing and ended up doing the opposite on a macro scale. So what made you want to do what you do and create what you create?

Steven: Well, there’s a lot of different ways that I want to incorporate humans in technology. I think the first is just a sense of wonder and honestly chafing a little bit about the limitations of the human body and brain, like, “This sucks.” Yes, there are many ways in which we are incredible. And I am certainly not a pessimistic person. I’d never understood this kind of nihilism. We’re incredible creatures, however you look at us. However, we got some pretty obvious problems that don’t fit well with modern society. So I am inspired by the macro problems of attention and motivation and feeling anxious all the time.

But also I’ve been working on this on my own. Often it’s just pen and paper systems, spreadsheets, those kinds of tools, things you can say or read back to yourself. I’ve been building that kind of stuff for myself for, I don’t know, since I was a teenager. So I have ADHD, although I wasn’t diagnosed until about 6 years ago. And I realized just how much hyperactivity, impulse control, and attention deficit had impacted the way I learned, how hard life was, how I motivated myself. And I’d made makeshift solutions for a very long time. And I figured out roughly how to be productive and happy despite a lot of obstacles. Usually it’s my own mind.

And so I’ve been forced to understand my own behavior. And then at some point I realized, “Oh, I’m not alone in this. Other people have these problems.” The more I mentioned it or the more I would hand out just simple stuff, whether it’s advice or basic systems, people would use it and found it useful. “Maybe I could do something more with this.” And then I have more speculative stuff, of course, that is not so straightforward.

Built for myself

Jack: So Threads, I believe that was your first piece of technology, right?

Steven: It’s the first one that I have released that isn’t like a prototype or a piece of test software.

Jack: And that was just a version, like you said, of something you’d built for yourself to help with your attention and productivity. Is that correct?

Steven: Yeah, it’s exactly that. I built pretty much all of this for myself. That’s what motivates me. It’s very hard to get excited about a problem that I don’t share. But honestly, I was trying to build something much more complicated and I was frustrated and like, “This is what I need. I need something to help me manage my working memory, because I can’t hold all these things in my head just over the span of a day. I don’t need a to-do list to know what to do next week. I need something that just helps me know what I’m supposed to be doing right now, maybe what’s next, and just manage the actual process of in-the-moment focus.”

It’s not even really about productivity so much as it is I need to get stuff out of my head so I can do one thing. Because if I have to remember that I need to do that, or somebody’s interrupted me, or I interrupt myself, I just lose a train of thought. So I needed a very simple system to just hold on to, almost like sticky notes. “Here’s what I’m doing right now. Here’s what I’m doing next.” To manage that process. And that’s all it is.

Jack: So the idea is you’re writing stuff down to empty your head of thinking about it so that you can fully focus on the task that you want to tackle. Is that correct?

Steven: Yeah, there’s a lot of potential uses, but it is essentially a way to extend your short-term and working memory in a way that doesn’t get buried. You can do this stuff with pen and paper and reminders and alarms and Pomodoro. Those things get garbage collected, first of all, especially by people with ADHD, because of low object permanence. That you can’t see it, it stops existing. Literally, it’s not that you can’t remember, it’s that it no longer exists in your mind until you see some indication, “Oh, that thing exists.” And so because neurodivergent people tend to live in the environment they’re in, that’s all that exists.

You go off on a tangent and you never come back, or you become overwhelmed and burdened, literally stressed out by the number of things you’re trying to remember, what you’re supposed to be doing, and it degrades everything you do. It feels bad. “Oh, I’ll just check my e-mail real quick. And oh, that looks interesting. I want to get to that later today.” But I don’t want to hold on to it. I don’t want to remember it. And that’s what we do, is we try to hold these things in our head and then we end up dropping them. We forget that we’re supposed to do something and it becomes too difficult.

Some reason to care

Jack: Have you ever read Rick Rubin’s book The Creative Act: A Way of Being?

Steven: I have it right over there. I literally just bought it yesterday, though, so I’ve only read like half a chapter.

Jack: What you’re saying about creating something just for yourself is very in alignment with that book. He says that good art is made for the creator and that the audience comes last. When you make something specifically for yourself, people are going to empathize with it. They’re going to relate.

What else do you read?

Steven: I do read a fair amount of nonfiction, but it is kind of like work to read it. I also read a lot of fantasy. I like writing fantasy, a little bit of science fiction. That’s a lot more fun for me. Most of the reading that I do is topical. It’s highly relevant to what I’m interested in now, and I don’t do a lot of just because non-fiction reading, because I won’t apply it to my life and I won’t remember it. I have to have some reason to care.

Jack: Give me an example of a book you’ve read recently.

Steven: I finished Scarcity Brain recently, the Michael Easter book, the second one. It’s a very good book. I don’t know. I don’t do memory. I can go look at the stack over here. Fantasy, I was reading James Butcher, Jim Butcher’s son. His books are great. One second. Here we go. I got Iain McGilchrist’s The Matter with Things. That’s a neuroscientist who dabbles in philosophy. I’m sure you’d be interested in him. He’s a fascinating person.

He’s looking at split-brain patients. So they’ve had their corpus callosum, the thing that joins your left and right hemispheres, severed because they have seizures, and they end up behaving a lot like two different people. And it allows us to measure the massive differences in the way the left and right hemisphere see the entire world, and through that begin to understand more about the nature of reality. Which is a fascinating twist, that he basically uses perception and studies from what schizophrenic patients are reporting to try to understand the very nature of reality itself, which is interesting spin. So he’s got a lot to say. It’s a dense read. It’s not for everybody, but I think that there’s a lot of truth, definitely on the frontier, but a fascinating person. Maybe listen to an interview first.

Jack: I’m going back and I’m rereading some of the old fiction that I’ve read and really shaped how I look at the world. Right now I’m about to finish rereading Siddhartha. I find myself very drawn to these books that don’t just cultivate knowledge, they cultivate a feeling, a way of being.

Steven: I think a novel is an important format for communicating ideas because it forces you to go slow and sequentially and to think and take your time and go back and look. It’s completely different from video and even podcast. It is like a distillation of somebody’s life or most important ideas all in one perfect little package. And what a gift that we can read these things and pick these up from other people.

Jack: I had a conversation with a friend on the podcast a few weeks ago, and he said to me that he thinks myth and fiction is so powerful because of the empathy that we have for the characters in it.

Steven: There’s something to that for sure. Most of the best-selling or really impactful nonfiction books contain lots of narrative as is, but also, I mean, we’re kind of talking about the same thing. This is another strategy: when I read things that are topical to my interests, my own life story is the thing that gives me emotion. When I see somebody say something, it will immediately resonate because I’ll see myself in it or my problems or a problem I care about, and that is what causes the feeling. But I don’t think that’s the only way. I think it matters that there’s some meaning or deep emotion, some sort of resonance there. It doesn’t necessarily have to be a fictionalized story to get there. We make stories up all the time. So as long as it inspires some emotion.

The other edge

Jack: You seem like you’re a very deep thinker. You’re very present. You’re conscious of the words that you’re saying. You seem like a very conscious person. How has this impacted you in building a product and building a life? What’s a blessing and a curse?

Steven: I think that it helps with reflection. It is the source of all of my ability to reflect and improve and be anti-fragile and understand what behavior, influences, caused me pain or problems, or are working, or making me feel better or worse. So this sort of conscientiousness and ability to reflect and be calm enough and observant enough to see the patterns and do something with them is simultaneously one of the things that is most valuable in my career or business or any of my creative works, just being alive.

But the other edge of the sword is over-contemplation, excessive rumination, paralysis, either by analysis or by the inability to step outside yourself and see you’re in a sort of cognitive trap. It is easy to overcomplicate things when you have the ability to sift through patterns and understand abstractions well. You can get lost in it very easily. And it sounds silly from the outside. It’s like, “Just go get up and work out. You don’t need to overthink this or have a big plan or get lost in your thoughts or any of that.” But it’s not easy to see from the inside. So I would say it is mostly a positive thing, but it does have drawbacks.

Jack: When you’re prone to rumination and overthinking, if you’re a very good articulator, you can almost talk yourself out of doing something good for yourself. I woke up at like 5:00 AM and ran every single morning a few summers ago, and I remember on the hard mornings it would be like this voice in my head very clearly articulating some seemingly very good points on why I shouldn’t do the thing. And I wonder if people without that ability to think have the same problems.

Steven: Yes, there is a dark side to different kinds of intelligence, of which there are many kinds. In this case you have verbal intelligence, because it can be turned on yourself, and not always in a good way, as many people can relate. Your own mind is often your worst enemy and biggest critic, and, as we’ve talked about and I’m sure will continue to, is not always on your side, meaning holistically, long-term thinking, and often is completely opposed to what is good for you.

And it is not great to have that turned on you, or to not stop either. Generally speaking, either my mind is going or my body is going. It has to be one or the other. One of them will be going at all times. So that is another drawback, is then what has made me good at thinking and speaking is that it never stops, almost never stops. So that’s the drawback, is that that’s one of the reasons you get good at things is because you obsessively or incessantly do something.

An active thing

Jack: Before we started talking, you said that you meditate about an hour a day, which I really respect. Has that meditation helped you with calming your mind and building a quieter home inside?

Steven: I would say it’s a bigger — it’s hard to say because it’s different — bigger effect than actual ADHD medication has been on my life. It completely changes the way you see the world. I started out, I think, as you mentioned, like you or many others, where it’s a sporadic habit. It’s like, “I should eat healthy,” or, “I should fix this part. I should do this thing.” It’s just another thing to tack on that is mildly good for you. And for a lot of years, that’s where my meditation habit was.

But after reading Michael Singer’s book The Untethered Soul, and then I read his autobiography, I had no idea that you could actually do real exploration of the inner space. You could understand consciousness in the world and what is happening just by sitting there, or have transcendental experiences, experience things that there’s no other way to experience. I had no idea how much was possible through meditation, that it wasn’t just sitting there waiting for something, that it was an active thing. And that inspired me to do more. And then I slowly increased the time.

But the level of clarity and just patience, the ability to not immediately react, which is incredibly valuable when you’re an impulsive person, goes up significantly. There’s a half-life, of course, doesn’t last forever. It allows you to step outside of your own emotions and thoughts, not only calm them down and quiet them down to 0 when you get good at it, but it allows you to step behind them and carry that forward into everyday life in such a way that, I don’t know, you don’t take it too seriously when you start to feel depressed or panicked or worried or angry. It’s like watching somebody else be angry. You still feel it, but you don’t take it too seriously because it doesn’t feel like you anymore. So that’s what it’s done, in a nutshell.

Time for quiet

Jack: What I’ve found is that as I’ve gotten further and further away from my meditative state, my calm, I’ve become less productive. And so building that meditation practice and building quiet time into my day to reflect and to just be is something that is actually more productive than anything that I’ve done. Have you gone through any points in your life where you had to convince yourself of the same thing?

Steven: Yes. I’d say that now, having had enough practice with this — I don’t know how many I’m at. I’ve done 1000 hours at least, I’d guess. I mean, that’s not world-class, but it’s no longer amateur. So I’d say it’s different now. This is like having been a long-distance runner for 10 years. And then you go do something else and you get out of shape and somebody asks you whether long-distance running is worth it. And you sort of inherently deep down understand how valuable it is. It’s been fried into my circuitry how good this is. So it’s easy to pick back up again. It’s not like a thing I should do. I fall out of the habit like anyone else. But once you’ve seen the impact on your life, especially long term, it’s not quite as hard.

But you mentioned something else that was useful there, which is just time for quiet and to be alone and reflect. I think that that is actually a much more approachable set of advice for most people, is to just allow their default mode network to turn on, rather than trying to sit down and make yourself focus or not focus or whatever your style of meditation is, a bit too much of an ask. Actually just making a habit of a 30-minute walk is significant. It’s a big portion of it.

You need to allow the part of your brain that does garbage collection and solves your problems and processes your feelings, decides how it feels about itself and your life and what you’re doing, comes up with ideas, to just run. If you’re suppressing it, talk about all the ways to get suppressed by modern life, you feel deep anxiety and a loss of identity and a loss of ability to choose, and that plagued a lot of people. So basically just go for a walk. Just sit there and be bored for about 30 minutes a day or more, ideally in silence, literally doing nothing, and your life will improve significantly if you can do it.

The chair test

Jack: How did you start out? I have many friends, some with ADHD, that have trouble being quiet and not having any stimuli. So how did you get over that initial hump?

Steven: Yeah, attention, much like productivity, is sort of like a distraction. It’s really like a downstream effect, that attention gets destroyed. It really does have more to do with your level of stimulation need, kind of like not paying down a debt. The longer you go without paying it down, the worse it feels. So a lot of people are to a point where they can’t fall asleep without music or show playing in their ears or watching it, or they can’t not have sound playing. They can’t do a chore without sound playing. This comes from basically a low-level baseline dopamine and a bunch of other neurological changes. You can’t sit still.

So one of the things I recommend to people, I wrote this down so I wouldn’t forget the name, is the chair test, which is relevant. It is basically, if you can’t sit in a chair for 5 minutes and do nothing or just look at a wall, you are not OK. And that is a quick heuristic that I use in my life. If I can’t do that or I am resisting doing that, that alone is a sign that I’m not OK on the inside, and anything I do on the outside is only going to make it worse or to kick the problem down the road.

So there’s an answer how to get over it, but it changes the framing to “I’m not OK.” But you will rebalance your pleasure-pain systems. That’s from Dopamine Nation, the book. You will rebalance the systems literally just by letting yourself sit there. So if you will just sit there in a chair for probably about 5 minutes, maybe longer depending on how long it’s been, and just feel the pain — kind of like an ice bath or something, it is good pain — you will find that it subsides and then you’re just mildly bored or OK, however long you have to sit there, and that the longer you put it off, you’re actually making it worse and you’re also depriving yourself of actual pleasure.

It’s like the 50th of ice cream. You don’t really even feel it anymore. If you would like to actually taste it again, you have to stop eating ice cream for a little while and just feel terrible. Just crash. You just have to crash. You should think of pleasure as being conserved.
