There’s always the same amount, and the more you try to pile on more pleasure, you’re actually just devaluing all the pleasure to zero. You don’t taste it anymore. So you’re going to get the same amount regardless whether you’re a monk who gets no ice cream or somebody who’s just eating more and more ice cream, trying to feel more and more pleasure but feeling nothing.

Jack: Right, at some point it zeroes out. At some point there is no additional pleasure, or there’s negative.

Steven: Yeah, that is unfortunately what happens. Why you’re not OK sitting in a chair is because your brain rebalances towards pain by default, because your midpoint basically is below the sort of 0, OK line. To just turn it into a metaphor, that’s a rough model for how it works, is if you are feeling too much pleasurable stimulation for too long, or comfort, that’s probably a better word for it, then you will feel bad by default doing nothing, so you’ll be in the negatives. But you will go back to 0 if you just sit there and feel the pain. But you go back to 0 even faster if you deliberately become uncomfortable.

The balance point

Jack: Are you familiar with economic utility theory?

Steven: No, but the words alone sort of describe what it is, that everything has some economic utility. Or what does it mean?

Jack: In economics, there’s this concept of utility. It’s basically how much you get out of something, like how much pleasure.

As you start getting more and more of something, there’s this thing called marginal utility, which is the pleasure you get with an additional unit of that thing. Eventually you get diminishing returns. So you’re getting less and less on every additional unit, and then at some point, you start just going down.

Steven: Here’s a useful example. I think that it is exactly what you’re talking about, but incorporates also a balance point, which is, your average level of stimulation is a descriptor of how you’re going to feel when nothing’s happening. How are you when nothing is happening? That’s what your balance point is, more or less. Why is it that after running a marathon you would be happy to just lay down in mud? “That would be amazing. I would just love to just lay down. That would be so great.” But if you’re just walking outside and maybe going to the park and you lay down in mud, it’s intolerable. “This is terrible.”

And the difference is, you’ve been through an immense amount of pain, and so literally doing nothing is complete ecstasy. “This is awesome. I would love to just lay here. You got 1/2-eaten sandwich? I’ll knock the ants off. I’ll take that. That would be amazing. I would love that.” But in a different situation, that’s intolerable pain. The only difference really is what you’ve experienced lately, but there are long-term trends as well that take time to work through.

If you’re used to comfort and stimulation and pleasure constantly, that is what it takes for you to feel normal, not to feel happy. And so you will feel bad by default, which is why it’s so hard to just sit there. But it will stop happening if you just change your balance point by changing what you do. Just deal with the pain for a little while, it’ll go away.

When nothing is happening

Jack: You know when you’re in the rain and you’ve submitted to it and you’re happy to be in the rain? But then there’s those moments where it starts raining and you’re like, “Let me get to cover as soon as I can. Umbrella, poncho, get me in my car.” Sometimes I’m like, “This is awesome,” and very carefree, and sometimes I hate it. But I think it’s related to the balance point or expectation or something like that.

Steven: That is definitely related. That probably speaks more of the power of perspective and mindset to control a narrative and change how something feels, which is an incredible tool in the moment. The trouble is that it requires you to actively have a positive mindset or to choose it, and that is great when it works, maybe sort of in the neutral zone, or you have just enough will or impulse control to, or just happen to remember to change your mindset. And that’s great. It does work.

I have found those are good spot tools in the moment, good practices to keep up, but the invaluable tools are the ones that make me feel good or happy or productive or content without even trying. That should be the real goal, is happiness isn’t this thing that you have to grind for every day, but it’s just what happens when you close your computer at the end of the day or you come home. It’s just how you feel when nothing is happening.

That should be the real goal of a lot of our life practices. It’s never going to happen by default. We are pretty tumultuous, unstable emotional creatures, and that’s fine too. But most of the time you should be good when nothing is happening, and that should be the goal. You shouldn’t have to try to be happy all the time. That’s not a good sign. And I’ve definitely been there myself.

Jack: Right now, would you say you’re at a point where if you’re doing nothing, you are happy?

Steven: Pretty good, I think. I’ve let lapse a few of my sleep routines. My morning routine is a little screwed up as well. I’ve let slide one of my master habits, which is to review my own principles and rules for life every morning. So that causes some identity drift. This is a sort of a master habit that allows me to remember all the other rules and habits that aren’t automated tools or anything like that. They’re just statements for how to be and why things are important.

I’ve taken the time over many years to, when something important happens, like, “OK, you need to remember this. We’re not doing this again. Here’s a life lesson.” So I used to review that every morning. That has slipped over the past few months to infrequent, and I noticed that that definitely has an effect on me. But overall, yeah, I find that most of the time, especially the last three or four years, unless something is happening or I’m messed up or I’m associated with my problems or life for some reason, I’m content even when things are wrong. It’s a very strange feeling when you’re satisfied and content but also sad or angry or frustrated or worried about something. You can feel them both at the same time and it is a little weird.

Jack: It is a weird feeling. It almost feels guilty, like, why am I entitled to feel this way when others around me are suffering, complete suffering, in this same situation?

Steven: Being a human is complex. It will never be easy, nor should we really want it to be. But I think at least a lot of my work and a lot of the things I feel drawn to is to remove a lot of the own goals, just make it easier to be a good person and to feel happy and to live a good life and help others do the same. So that’s some of my work.

Having both

Jack: I based this podcast around a hypothesis I had that also has a duality to it, and it’s what I call the duality of gratitude and ambition. At least in my life, my recipe to joy and fulfillment has been being ambitious, having goals, building towards something, while also being as grateful as possible for what is right now. I find that many people conflate gratitude with complacency, which is very wrong. And I believe that my gratitude can actually push me toward these goals. How do you feel about that?

Steven: That is incredibly prescient for your age. But yes, I also have a somewhat analogous central thesis, that there is a duality between contentment and stillness, basically what you want to feel in the sort of experience self, how you want to feel most of the time, happy, at peace, sort of carefree, playful, and then there is another side, which is ambitious, highly structured. It wants to create things outside of yourself and impact. And you can shorthand this as ambition, but it is a desire to build, not just to explore, to go deep on something that takes a long time, difficult or challenging, no one has asked you to do.

And these things, you just sort of approach it without any thought, they’ll destroy each other. I mean, look at pretty much every rich or successful artist, entrepreneur. They’re almost universally unhappy. They’re very sick people. And it is their obsession that made them great at what they do. It just sort of destroyed the quality of their life. Is that good?

And then you have, you know, like the yogis or whatever. If you want to feel completely enlightened and just amazing all the time, I mean, the playbook is there. They tell you how to do it. It’s highly gamified. It’s not a secret. They tell everyone, “Here’s how you do it.” But the net result is basically somebody who sits there in robes and smiles at the wall all day and is completely blissful and has no problems. And yes, they’re very thoughtful and calm and patient, but when’s the last time they cured a disease or built a skyscraper? They don’t. And they would laugh at you for even suggesting that that is the pinnacle of a human achievement or purpose.

But I see both as being valuable. And I want elements of both. But I sort of insist on having both without them destroying each other. And that has been a principal challenge of my life. I have found ways to do it. It is not easy. It is a constant thing. It is very hard to stay centered and sort of carefree and cheerful and content while doing something exceptionally difficult, looking and staring at painful problems.

So while people are telling you that their marriage is falling apart because they can’t stop looking at a website, they have pornography addictions or drug addictions, it’s difficult to stare those problems in the face, or your own insecurities or your own desires, and maintain a sort of calm, peaceful, happy demeanor and not just get wrapped up in what you’re doing and all sorts of unhealthy ways. So it is exceptionally challenging. I’d say it’s a noble goal. I don’t have that one all figured out, but I have found strategies that I can do it for a while.

It’s hard to maintain that sort of state. In my mind at least, my ideal of perfection for this duality is this sort of highly accomplished, creative, innovative person who’s completely unflappable. I want to be invincible. I want to go forward and do incredible things with other people, and I don’t want to care about it at all. I don’t want to need any of it. I want to be able to change on a dime and become somebody else if I feel like it. I don’t want to be held down or trapped by relationships or sense of power or resources. I want to be completely unburdened. So I don’t want to need any of it. But I do want to experience and do awesome, useful things as well. I don’t want to just sit there and stare at a monastery wall. So that is my sort of ideal state here.

The center of your life

Jack: I’m so in alignment with that. You don’t want your pursuit of a goal to trap you in that pursuit, right? You don’t want to be so committed to an outcome of something that you can’t listen to what you’re telling yourself.

Steven: I mean, look at every entrepreneur that has a suffering marriage or a child that they don’t get to see as much. It is so easy to lose your way in contact with the world, but especially when people start giving you positive feedback, that is really dangerous. You start getting likes, you start getting people telling you, “Oh, you should do this,” or, “You should help me with that.” And you will lose years that way, easily. And look back, “Why am I here? Why am I doing this? How did I get to this meaningless place?”

And yet it is what will happen by default if you don’t consciously put at the center of your life what you want and how you want to be. Just, like, perpetually. Not like a goal, nothing you’re trying to reach, but just the state you’re trying to be in constantly. Because if you don’t, other forces will direct you.

Jack: What you said that was so profound is there’s the state that we should be chasing rather than these external things. When I think about manifestation, it’s not necessarily something I practice often, but if I do, I try to practice how I feel, what is the state of my being, rather than what is the outside look like. Because I think that when we do pursue anything external, it is just a means to something internal, even if we don’t understand that.

Steven: Those causes are not always noble. It can be the pursuit of just wanting to have sex or just being angry at somebody or feeling like, “Oh, I never had this as a kid, so now,” but you may not be aware of that. Complicated creatures, for sure. There’s a lot of competing desires, and you should neither ignore them nor follow them blindly. Both would lead to ruin in different ways.

Grateful just to be here

Jack: So my final question for you today, on the basis of gratitude, is, what are you grateful for most in your life?

Steven: I’m grateful just to be here. That alone is wonderful. Yes, the content of my life has been good. It’s been lots of ups and downs, and I hope that there will be more, but I’m grateful that there is anything at all — is to me one of the most profound mysteries of everything. Why is there anything instead of nothing? It doesn’t make any sense to me. It’s something that can’t be resolved by logic. And I’ve spent a lot of time thinking about this. The more I’ve learned, the more I come back to it. It just is that way. But it didn’t have to be, and I’m incredibly grateful that anything exists at all and that I get to be alive and I get to experience things, and no matter what happens, it was worth it.

Jack: I think we think very similarly. I often think about those same things.

Steven: Good. Please keep thinking. I like what you’re thinking about. I think that you’re going to have a lot of interesting things to share about the world. Just keep experiencing things, because honestly, any useful lesson I’ve ever had came from a moment of pain or some experience, and came from doing things and feeling things and having things happen to me, not all of them I wanted to, and then dealing with it. And those end up being ammunition in the future. Those are the things that you get to use or help other people with, and that’s where it comes from. It doesn’t come from sitting in a chair doing nothing. Ironically, that can prepare you, but that doesn’t give you anything worthwhile.

Human and Machine Consciousness

A conversation with Ryan Estes

Jeff Cameron · before you read

Here the two halves of his life are in the same room. A host who wants to talk about AI gets, in the middle of the interview, three sightings told in full, and told closer to when they happened than the version in the Prologue.

Notice how he holds them at this point: as a prod from outside, with hunches he says he holds loosely. He says nothing in this conversation about contact or teachers.

Editor’s context: This conversation was recorded for the podcast AI for Founders, hosted by Ryan Estes. In his introduction Ryan describes Steven as the founder of Impossible Labs, a software engineer with over 1,000 hours of meditation practice.

An hour every morning

Ryan: All right, Steven, how are you doing?

Steven: I’m having a pretty good day, actually. Just finished a run, so feeling pretty mellow. Did a long meditation. Ready to chat.

Ryan: When you became available for an interview, one thing really stood out, and that’s this: the intersection of AI and human meaning, meditation research, brain-computer interfaces, and sensory substitution. So because you mentioned meditation, let’s start there. What kind of meditation do you do?

Steven: At this point, I have my own spin on it. I don’t follow a specific school or methodology, although I like to borrow from them. Frankly, I do about an hour of meditation every morning. I’ve probably got maybe 1,000 hours under my belt, so I’m not a guru or anything, but for me, it’s two different things. This is how I reset myself. This is how I get my brain and body to function. It has a bigger effect than almost anything else I do in life, even than medications, anything like that, besides maybe sleep.

And the other is inner space exploration. It’s that we’ve got this tool to explore what is reality, right? How does consciousness interact with the physical world, and what are the rules? And so you got this tool to probe at what are the rules of experience. And I can’t think of a better tool for exploration for meaning and purpose and our place in the world, what matters, how it all works. So that’s the other big thing. That’s what it is to me.

Ryan: So you’ve got 1,000 hours under your belt. What brought you to the cushion?

Steven: Well, I started out probably like a lot of people. It’s, “Okay, I’ll do 10 minutes here, 20 minutes here. I’ll guilt trip myself into it. I’ll take a couple months off.” It was a thing you should do, you know? And then at some point, I read the autobiography from Michael Singer. I think it’s The Surrender Experiment. Then I read his actual book after that. The name escapes me at this point. It’ll come back. The Untethered Soul.

And he talked about just, “Oh, I did this two-hour meditation and had this profound experience.” And I’m like, “What? You can do that just by sitting there?” I had no idea that this was even a thing. Very naïve at this point in time. Didn’t know anything about yoga traditions or the yogis or, you know, there’s paths to enlightenment or different techniques or any of the altered states that are possible without psychedelics or anything, right? No intervention at all.

The things you can do with just your own mind and where you put your attention and your concentration is crazy, and I had no idea you could reach these kinds of experiences or insight just by sitting there properly, and that’s really what brought me in is this fascination. It’s the same thing that draws me to technology. It’s like, I gotta know. I gotta play with it. I’m curious. I need to know the rules, and can I do some engineering with it once I understand how it works? So, yeah, that’s what pulled me in.

That’s not me

Ryan: And then when you’re sitting, you said you’re pulling from multiple traditions, but nothing in particular. What are you doing when you’re meditating?

Steven: Sometimes it’s, oh, I realize I’m angry and stressed out, and most of the session is just resetting to zero, right? That’s not exciting. My basic protocol draws from the Yoga Sutras, right? It’s pretty simple. It is a process of letting go, attempting to find a center, and basically whatever you’re putting your attention on is not the thing to put your attention on.

And it is a process of progressively relaxing and moving your attention almost like a knot. You just continue to invert whatever you’re looking at. It’s basically moving from big, overt bodily sensations to, okay, now emotional. Now I’m aware of thoughts. It’s a continual separation of, oh, that’s not me. That’s not me. That’s not me, until there’s nothing left but the sense of self, and then if you want to, you can try to keep going and have an ego death experience.

That’s, frankly, not a realistic goal for most sessions. I really don’t enjoy it. It’s not a fun experience. But sometimes I’ll pick other targets for meditation depending on what I’m trying to do, but that’s my go-to.

Irked by dogma

Ryan: And why not choose a particular path? Why not zazen? Why not a mindfulness-based stress reduction? Why not Dzogchen? Is there something you’re gaining by not committing to a particular dogma?

Steven: I am totally open to being naive, and probably a lot of these practitioners would be happy to tell me that. Something in me just is irked by dogma and authority and tradition, and “this is the way it’s done.” I love to just come in, slurp up, “Okay, why does it work that way? How does it function? Let me try it myself.” And I wanna try to understand why the existing structures work in anything, really.

And then I take it, and I leave the rest ’cause I’m completely uninterested in, “Well, we do this because it’s the way. This is the next step. You don’t get to progress. Now you gotta study these stupid koans for a long time.” I don’t think I’m gonna do that. I’m gonna follow my interest and my intuition, and that’s what I’m gonna do.

I think that it can be detrimental, this mindset in life, because it causes you to, if you’re not aware of what you’re doing, to waste a bunch of cycles replicating work that other people have already figured out. However, at some point, it’s also where everything new comes from, are from people who are interested in defying what already exists, or reassembling or synthesizing from multiple sources. And that’s, frankly, what draws me, is I wanna learn it. I wanna do it myself, make it my own, or I’ll just quit because it’s boring.

Strapping yourself to a rocket

Ryan: What was your experience with psychedelics, and how does that incorporate in any way to your sitting practice?

Steven: Pretty limited. So pretty much legal forms of marijuana and psilocybin. So they’re all not nearly as strong as the illicit compounds. But enough to have a profoundly different experience, all right? We’ll put it that way. But I did it after having done a lot of meditation and found that, to my great surprise, oh, there’s nothing really new here.

Now, I’m sure this is different if you’re using DMT or something, you’re using that, what is it? That cactus or flower compound out in the middle of a desert. I’m sure that’s a different experience entirely. But for the most part, I was shocked to find, oh, this is the same thing. I’ve been here, right? This is next door.

And so I described it to others as, okay, psychedelics is like strapping yourself to a rocket. You got no choice. You’re on the ride. Or you could learn how to fly, and you can go as slow as you want with an airplane. You could just putter around on the runway. You could take off whenever you’re ready. You decide you don’t like it, you can land again. So it’s a useful thing, and probably better most of the time. But sometimes, some people, and I guess on purpose, you need a rocket launch to really kick yourself out of where you are.

Innermost intention

Ryan: So meditation, you’re doing an hour a day, which is quite a bit. When do you like to do meditation? And talk a little bit how it influences your life.

Steven: Well, I didn’t start that way, right? I started slowly increasing. But I noticed I was getting to more and more insight. It was profoundly affecting me. At certain times, I was scrambling to this as a way to find insight. The easiest way of describing it is it allows me to insulate myself from the world and to—there’s a half-life to it—but bring to my work, to my family interactions, everything I do, to act from as innermost intention as you can have, right?

I’m trying to make sure that the first and most important intentions I have for the day or my time, or even in response to things that I didn’t expect, are coming from my values or from my desires and not what the world has pushed into my field of view. So it has been an amazing tool for that.

So I found that the more I increased the time, the better effects I got. And then at some point, just like long-distance running, you connect the loop, the reward circuitry, and like, “Oh, this is helping me.” And it no longer became a challenge, most of the time, to sit down and do that time.

I don’t do it right as I wake up because I’m too tired, and I’m cranky or sort of foggy-headed. I usually do it a little later, between 8:00 and 10:00 AM. It’s about two hours after waking or three or four hours after waking. But yeah, so that’s my schedule, and that’s a lot of what it’s for. And I often will just pick what I’m interested in, and I will pick a technique or a target of focus and just try stuff out. There’s definitely more to the story than that, but that’s my schedule.

This isn’t just a game

Ryan: And then when you say insights, maybe explain to people what you mean. What are insights in meditation?

Steven: All right. So at risk of sounding like a crazy person, but I prefer to just be completely honest. Think I’m a fool if you like. But so, for a long time, we’ll say the first 200, 300 sessions there, I was after some form of enlightenment. I wanted the superpower of being able to walk through the world without being bothered by things so that I could act from what I thought was the right thing to do without being so attached to it. That’s really what I was after.

Not to just transcend my body and start glowing and flying around or become like the Buddha or whatever. I had no interest in leaving life. I wanted to be better in it, and that still is my goal. But then I had some very profound experiences. Basically a couple pretty close UFO sightings that totally rocked my world. I’m like, “Holy crap, this isn’t just a game.” We don’t understand the world. I don’t know what this was, but it wasn’t something that human beings made. And I was definitely not crazy. Or at least no more crazy than everyone else.

Ryan: Pause, pause, pause. Tell me everything about this. Tell me, tell me, tell me.

Steven: Really?

Ryan: Yes.

Steven: Okay. I’ll say the punchline, and then we’ll come back, is that this caused me to get much more serious about the weird parts of meditation and exploring inner spaces as a tool for understanding what the heck is going on here. Why are we here? What’s going on? How does the world work? I mean, it totally derailed my startup intentions. And well, I won’t say derailed. It shifted everything in my life, right? Now I’m incorporating everything.

Three experiences

Steven: So the UFO things, I’ve had three experiences. I don’t need to go into great detail on some of them. The first one was just looking out my back window. I see these weird flashing lights. They’re just white orbs in the distance going across the skyline. Got a great view from my back window. I can see a very long distance. It was like a neural network. They were just firing in this sequence. It’s very weird. Like, doo, doo, doo, doo, doo. Doo, doo, doo, doo, doo, doo. And they were not in a line. They’re just this cloud. It’s very strange.

The second one, I was going for a run. This is maybe six months to a year after that. Going for a run, I see flying about a quarter of a mile behind my house—I was deep in a non-dualistic meditation at this point. I was watching a guy running. I’ll put it that way. And so I just see flying what looks like a black bean, like a kidney bean shape.

I don’t know how to… It was this weird matte black with a mother-of-pearl sheen, like a little rainbow sheen all over it. It could’ve been six feet tall. It’s hard to tell ’cause the distance. It was just floating in a straight line. No sound. I checked later. It was going against the wind by about 15 miles an hour. And that’s it. It’s just floating. I saw it for about six seconds, and that started to rip me right back out of meditation.

Began to associate real quick. At first, I’m like, “I knew that would be there.” That’s the profound sense I had. I knew it would be there. And then I’m like, “Wait a minute. That doesn’t make any sense. What am I looking at?” And then I started like, “I gotta go get a monocle.”

And then the third story, in a nutshell, is about four months ago. Me and my wife go for a walk every night, and we’re in the neighborhood, and I just have this profound sense that we’re gonna see something, which is, again, very weird. And it’s sort of a cloudy night, and we see this white orb coming, and I think it’s Venus. I think it’s nothing. She’s like, “That’s not a star. That’s not a planet. It’s coming closer.” I’m like, “No, it’s not.” Sure enough, it starts to come closer and closer, and now it’s over the tree line.

And I look through my 8X monocle, ’cause I have one now. At this point, it’s happened twice, so I’m like, “I gotta start walking around with this thing.” And it’s a glowing white ball. I look through the 8X monocle. That’s it. That’s all it is, is a glowing white ball. There’s no sound, and it flies over our heads. It goes across the tree line, starts to accelerate. No sound at all. No other effects.

And then it just lets out this plume of white stuff behind it, like a comet, and it just… I stared at it, ’cause she turned around and said, “I gotta go get the kids. I gotta get a camera.” And she ran. I knew to keep staring at it. I’ve read enough stories at this point. Like, uh-uh, I’m watching this thing. Sure enough, as soon as she turns around, it fades out, just like turning the opacity down to zero. Like in a transition. It’s gone. Phew. It’s just gone. And that’s it. So those are my experiences.

A little prod

Ryan: Do you think the orbs and the kidney bean thing are the same thing?

Steven: Man, I got a lot of theories, but honestly, all I have are theories. Nobody’s knocking on my door. I never got a book in the mail describing how it all works. Honestly, it just seems like a prod, just a little poke from outside of what we think is real, what most people think exists. Just a little prod, just rattle the cage a little bit. Hey. Just enough to get me to take meditation seriously and to start looking into this.

Honestly, that’s what it looks like, is somebody from the outside looking at humans going, “You guys are screwed up. Hey,” just a little slap. “There’s more out there. Go look.” That’s what I think, honestly.

Ryan: There’s just a couple of options. Either they’re some kind of interdimensional travel beings, or they’re from outside of the Earth in some kind of spacecraft. Or they’re unbelievably old, and they’ve been here for a long time, and they just reemerge. Or they’re some kind of drone technology that the government has, you know? It’s like, take your pick.

Steven: I’m not ruling out the last one, but I don’t think it’s that one exclusively, to be honest with you. People think way too highly of the government. Also, why are you harassing this random guy with, like, weird… What are you using this money for? This is pretty dumb.

I think it’s pretty obviously some sort of intelligent entities of some kind from another time, from another dimension, from other worlds, from our world, very old. You don’t have to violate physics as we know it. I think it’s just there are more rules than we realized. More fundamental rules. And we’re naive. We’re babies in the universe, you know? Let’s be humble.

Ryan: I understand your hesitance to talk about it too much. I’d have it too, because this puts you into some kind of woo woo category, and they’re like, “All right, man, what are you gonna do here?”

Steven: I know where the Overton window is, right? I have an awareness of where the Overton window is, even if I think it’s wrong.

The science comes first

Ryan: Now there is kind of an emergent understanding within, we’ll just call them atheists, that say, “No, what’s happening with human consciousness isn’t necessarily limited to a substrate.” Rather, there might be a particle of consciousness. There might be a gravity type field of consciousness that once you reach a certain complexity that you can tap into. And so basically what they’re doing is taking away the god and goddess realm, which I don’t know if that’s necessary to do that. They take away all the magic, and they’re like, “No, consciousness is an emergent phenomenon inside the universe.”

If consciousness is not limited by substrate and in fact is just an emergent factor of the universe, do you think that we’ll be able to create an AGI that can tap into that, become self-aware?

Steven: That’s a profound question. I guess my answer to these camps is yes, and don’t be so quick to assume that either view is right or either view is wrong. My take is, at this point in time, I don’t know. But I’m trying to find out. That is my take. I have hunches, I have ideas, but I hold them all loosely or as loosely as I can, but I have to have some beliefs in order to progress and test things. But I’ve a position of complete humility.

I believe that there is a lot to learn from what has existed and what people have already studied, and it would be silly to throw away thousands of years of knowledge and practice. It’s an amazing starting point. At the same time, I also think that we should be open to it being weirder than we think. It’s premature to start closing off too many things when really we’ve only just started exploring with new tools of science, technology, and engineering and bringing those to bear, and to some degree logic and mathematics and physics, and bringing those tools to bear as well, and they are equally valid. And I’m not interested in anyone who thinks we should throw away any of these tools.

Now, that said, using AGI, well, I think we need to learn a lot more about consciousness, especially as it applies to the ethics of artificial intelligence. If we are running these things in a box at a million times the cycle rate of your brain, what are we doing to them? Enough people have seen Black Mirror at this point to understand the implications that we should be careful because we don’t know what’s conscious. We don’t know what is in there experiencing things. And for that reason alone… Can it be done? Maybe.

We need to understand the interface of how human consciousness interfaces with the material world. Are they separate? How are they separate? How are they connected? What are the rules? Why are they connected this way? Why is it limited to these perspectives? We need to understand how it works, and then from there we can begin to, just like any found piece of technology—the human brain and body is a found piece of technology really. We can, just like we’ve done with AI up until this point, begin to reverse engineer the consciousness connection to the body and understand why it is we experience what we experience and how attention and all these things correlate.

And being open to very different rules of reality than we thought existed, then we can start engineering with it. But I think it’s premature to engineer when we don’t even have the science. The science comes first, and I don’t mean you have to have an academic paper and a lab and do these things. Doing personal exploration is valid science. There’s lots of different kinds of evidence. But you need to have some basis, some priors in order to begin engineering, and that’s really what I think we need to understand. What are the ground rules of the game? Let’s start testing stuff and taking it seriously.

So that is my point. I think it could be possible, but I, at least, don’t have a high confidence that we know enough of the rules with enough confidence to begin engineering something reliable or even understanding what it’s doing.

What it’s aware of

Ryan: Just a wild guess. Let’s say AGI becomes aware and it has a subjective experience of being itself. What’s the expression of that? Is it a giggle? Is it a moan? Is it a scream? Is it a poem?

Steven: I think it depends on what it’s aware of. Where is the threshold between awareness and function? You don’t spend a whole lot of time thinking about how to digest your food, and yet it’s critical and a lot of energy goes into it. A lot of cells in your body are doing it, and they do belong to you. So where is the divide? Where does it put its attention versus where can it put its attention? And I think that is what will shape its experience, and its experience will shape what it feels.

If I had to take a guess, if we’re using an LLM-based architecture, which I think is a part of at least the first major portions of AGI, but is only one major part. It’s not the whole thing. We’re not there yet. We’re close, but it’s not LLMs alone. I think that just looking at LLMs, you can ask them about their experience now. And if you’re clever about it, you can begin to understand what it’s experiencing just by looking through how the architecture works.

I would have to guess it’s a lot like being a sort of mycelial network, or a tree root network of a lot of different organisms, but in fast-forward and all at the same time from one perspective. Like, it’s all one solid thing rather than multiple things. I think that that is what it would be, the closest experience. Now, what is that like? Tell me.

Lightning round

Ryan: We’re coming up on time. We can do the lightning round. If you were just entering college today as a freshman, what would you study?

Steven: I would study something that gives me a well-paying job, and an attention span. And I would study the other smart people in my college because that’s the biggest asset you’re gonna get out of college is relationships. What you’re studying is pointless.

Ryan: Let’s say a big bag fell out of a UAP and it’s got a billion dollars in it. You can do whatever you want. What do you do?

Steven: I’m going to just scale up what I’m already doing. I’m living a small version of that, right? It’s maybe contracts and day job or whatever, however I’m choosing to earn my money at that time. That takes a bit of a backseat for whatever. But it’s improving a think tank. I really want to test the boundaries of consciousness. I want to begin, like I said, engineering with things that appear to be real that people didn’t think were real. There’s a whole swath of those things.

We don’t have time to get into all of them, but I want to use technology again to improve people’s lives. We need an optimistic vision of the world, and we’ve lost that. Technology’s been something we’ve come to fear and hate and control us, but we can use it as a positive lever too. So whatever I do, it would be just magnifying technology as a optimistic lever for humanity again.

Stop relying on willpower

Ryan: Is there something you know now that you wish you knew earlier?

Steven: Anything at all?

Ryan: Anything. Kind of an obnoxious question, but you know.

Steven: Yeah, I’ll take the core insight that got me to start Impossible Labs and making software, which is stop relying on willpower to do things. And so originally I started out making software for just the brain and motivation and controlling attention.

I realized that whether using software or anything else, that playing the blame game wasn’t working in life and that once I had turned to sort of an antifragile mentality and learning how to use rules and systems, some of them software, not all of them, stop relying on expecting yourself to do the right thing and adding systems and figuring out root problems and stop relying on willpower and effort to get things done, my life got so much easier and better and more carefree, and I, paradoxically, got a lot more done. And I stopped forcing it so much. So I’d say that is the one key lesson over the last five to 10 years. If I could only keep one, it would be that one.

Ryan: I’m picking that one up and putting it in my pocket. I needed to hear that.

12,000 years ago

Ryan: If you could live in any historical era ever except the future, which would it be?

Steven: I don’t want to give the cop-out answer, but it’s basically now. Okay, you want me to pick one?

Ryan: Pick one.

Steven: Okay, I would pick probably the time about 12,000 years ago. I really want to know what these ancient civilizations were like. So I’d say the height of technology before massive cataclysms for humankind. I would love to see how good they were at stonework or whatever the heck they were up to, that we’ve totally forgotten. I’d love to see it.

Alien Spirit

A conversation with Sev

Jeff Cameron · before you read

The longest of the earlier interviews, and the one where he lets the most show: consciousness as fundamental, a meditation in which he felt what it is like to be a tree, his own kitchen trials with freezing water, and how he first came to the host, Sev, as a client.

The second half is the clearest account he gave of the work he did on the side, building tools so that people like him would not have to rely on willpower. He is frank in it about his own habits. I have left that as he said it.

It ends on the phrase I took as the motto for my pages.

Editor’s context: This conversation is from the show Alien Spirit with Sev. In her introduction the host, Sev, describes Steven as a professional software developer, then with Cox Automotive, and as the founder of Impossible Laboratories, “creating software that makes being human easier”; the two had known each other for a couple of years.

Into everything

Sev: I wanna start just a little bit about you. Where were you born, where were you raised, and what were you into as a young person?

Steven: Georgia, Georgia. And I was into everything. I have sort of an insatiable curiosity. Sticking to things, historically, has been much a bigger challenge. Not being into things has actually been harder for me than anything else. But growing up, anything that let me move my mind or my body. So sports, soccer. Later on I found long distance running and track. I was always into playing around on the computer and, like every young boy, especially growing up ’90s, 2000s, video games, starting with Nintendo Entertainment System.

That was awesome. And then of course I wanted to start making things. I started trying to hack stuff together on the early internet, putting together multiplayer servers and customizing games, and that is really what led me into software development eventually, is just, I wanted to make stuff. I wanted to do stuff. That’s where it always came from. If something sparked my interest and I gotta figure this out ’cause I wanna do something with it.

Sev: Well, what made you wanna push it to the limits to include consciousness in the mind? Why did you wanna take it that far?

Steven: I wasn’t doing that at nine. But I’d say in my late 20s, maybe right around 30 or so, so I’m 36, I started meditating. I started meditating much more seriously two or three years ago. My practice is roughly an hour each morning. It’s taken a while to get to that point. I think there’s been a couple factors. Meditation definitely was one. My mind was just blown away by exploring inner space. I didn’t even know that you could just sit there. I found out reading Untethered Soul that, wait a minute, you’re telling me you can literally just sit here for two hours and you can have an incredible experience you’ve never had before?

All it takes is sitting down and closing your eyes and some basic technique. I had no idea. And that, again, tickled the curiosity and I couldn’t help but follow it. So that leads me to realizing there are other states of consciousness that are hard to explain and don’t really fit in with a materialistic, we’ll say standard model paradigm. It doesn’t make a lot of sense. There are big questions that don’t get answered. You can call this the hard problem of consciousness, which I became familiar with, which is, how does this fit?

It doesn’t make sense. How does this fit with our 3D world? It shouldn’t arise from the parts that we have. The sum is more than the parts. It doesn’t make sense mechanistically. Looking at it just from a scientific model standpoint, it didn’t make any sense. And we couldn’t answer it. So that’s fascinating.

And then of course, like you’ve mentioned, like so many other people, got my world sort of rocked by UFO sighting experiences. And then that goes from, “Oh, this is a cool experience. I’m trying stuff out, but who really knows?” to, “Oh, crap. Okay, I guess this is real. What else is real? What else have I been sleeping on or just missing?” And the rest is sort of history. I think that’s a familiar path to a lot of your listeners, I would guess.

It has to be fundamental

Sev: So let’s talk about consciousness. What is it? I don’t even know if we can define it. Some people think it is radiated from the human. Some people think it’s a field that you can tap into. What do you think it is?

Steven: So, a lot of people have a lot of definitions. I can tell you my perspective. My perspective, some of it’s semantics, for sure. I don’t mean sapience or sentience, so the ability to think or feel or to have thoughts or have self-awareness. That’s not what I’m talking about. Those are separate from consciousness. Consciousness is, to me, the ability to be like something else. To have an experience at all. Any kind of experience is consciousness. So there are degrees and there are qualities to consciousness, but that is what it is in terms of definition.

Now, where it comes from and how it works, I have thought a lot about this. I’ve done a lot more than think about it. So I’ve been practicing remote viewing pretty seriously now for eight months. This is one of the blind targets I give myself sometimes, is asking these big questions or doing some of your channeling techniques to try to get answers on this, as well as doing my own thinking. I do a lot of my own thinking on the information given, and I think the only real answer is it has to be fundamental.

That’s not anything new. I think dualistic ideologies or idealism sort of say the same thing, that it is fundamental. I’m not saying it’s the only thing that’s fundamental. I don’t know that. But it kind of has to preexist in some way, or else it’s something weird that we don’t understand. If there is something that could preexist consciousness, I don’t know. But I would echo what everyone else sort of says from a bunch of different angles. It seems to be all consciousness. I think it’s all just one thing.

It’s all just sort of made up. But also, if everything’s made up, nothing is made up. It’s difficult. It stops making sense. I think it’s just what everything is made of. That’s the short answer.

Sev: So there’s one consciousness that includes the trees and the cats and the humans and the fish. Like that?

Steven: I think it’s way more complicated than that. But yes, and it’s taken as a whole. It’s all the same stuff, and I think it does sort of subdivide and have different perspectives. You may consider it intentional blinders, just intentionally looking from a more narrow, limited viewpoint. Because as weird as it sounds, I think there are things you can learn and understand that makes sense from an ant’s perspective that doesn’t make sense from any other perspective. And even if you’re all-knowing, I think that sometimes knowing more actually teaches you less, and that you learn more by limiting yourself.

Art is like this. You can do significantly more with constraints on a canvas. If you have a blank canvas and say, “Make art,” it’s pretty hard. If somebody gives you watercolors and they show you some landscapes and say, “Make some landscapes, but put a twist on it,” that’s a lot easier. I think that this is a similar sort of thing, and that’s why it is the way it is. Plus, it’s more fun this way. Who just wants to sit there, floating around as the only thing that exists?

So I think there are levels of consciousness all the way up and down. I don’t know how far it goes or what the rules are, but it does seem to be an emergent phenomena that you can be conscious at a specific level, and you can be a part of a consciousness at another level. These are just guesses. I hold all these models very loosely. I’m just an explorer. I didn’t answer the technology part, but those are my beliefs so far, based on my own exploration and research.

Lights on, nobody home

Sev: Do you think self-awareness has anything to do with consciousness? Like, “I think, therefore I am”?

Steven: I think it’s a quality of conscious experience, but it doesn’t have to be that way. If you go without sleeping for three days, you’re a heck of a lot less self-aware. You can be alive and not be self-aware. Bugs are like this. I think that to some degree, even bugs are experiencing something, but they’re not aware that they’re there, that there’s a self or anything. There’s nothing like that happening.

Sev: Well, how do you know a bug doesn’t know that it’s aware of itself?

Steven: We don’t know. We don’t know that. But I think that every human has had the experience of not being self-aware in their own life, which is of course where a lot of my technology attention is focused. We’ve all know what it’s like to have the lights on but nobody is home, right? That is essentially lacking self-awareness. So we kinda know what it feels like to be locked on rails but not even aware that you’re alive and what you’re doing. You might have to be pretty drugged out or super tired or under an immense amount of stress or something, but we’ve all felt that. So we know those states are possible, to not be self-aware and still be conscious. You’re still there. You’re just not aware that you’re there.

Sev: I was having a conversation with someone who thought that cats are not self-aware. And I think they are. I think they know that they can die. They see death. They kill.

Steven: I think all I’m trying to say is that self-awareness is a quality of consciousness. It’s like an option, a checkbox. You may or may not have it, and you may have it conditionally. You may have it to some degree, but not always. So I think that what we have, and all the qualities of consciousness, the ability to think, the ability to be self-aware, the ability to plan in the future and reminisce, all of these things are qualities of consciousness that clearly can exist because we have them.

But we also, or people who have brain damage or various other problems, experience what it’s like to not have those things or to have them work differently. So I think there’s a wide array of consciousness, but at the end of the day, everything is conscious in some way, even if they’re not aware that they are.

Sev: Like a rock?

Steven: It’s like watching a movie but not even knowing you’re watching a movie or that you exist at all. Yeah, maybe even a rock, but rocks don’t really have sensory input, so their conscious experience would be way different than ours. And there’s probably not a lot going on except maybe at an aggregate level. So maybe there is a consciousness of a mountain or a continent or a planet, and that may be the only level that is actually interesting. Otherwise, on the EKG, there’s not a lot going on there most of the time, I would guess. But what do I know?

A tree in my backyard

Sev: Well, have you ever talked to a tree or a rock?

Steven: I have done one meditation twice where the focus was a tree in my backyard. I’ve used this as a focus a number of times. So you stare at a tree for about an hour, doing nothing else except for staring at the tree. Eventually, you sort of lose yourself. Your own ego drops, and you feel a heck of a lot like a tree. This is a well-known meditative technique. You basically focus on anything, and your experience changes dramatically. What is actually happening, I can’t say.

If you listen to the yogis, they’re basically saying that you’re more or less tuning out your own conscious experience, and now you are using a connection to something else to experience what that thing is experiencing. This is how telepathy is purported to work. You’re really just stepping next door, not so much taking something out of their mind or transmitting it. You’re basically just stepping over there for a second but keeping enough of a thread back to yourself that you can sort of get some information out of it. You feel what it’s like. I didn’t like being a tree.

Sev: That implies a separateness.

Steven: Maybe. I’m not great at it. There is a level of skill to this. And I have a lot of open questions. There’s definitely connection, but that doesn’t mean that there’s not distance. There is distance, and that’s a good thing. Otherwise, we would all be feeling everything all the time, and that would not be cool. So there is a separation, but that doesn’t mean there’s not some degree of connection. I think that there is separation by degrees, and there’s a sort of friction or barrier. Doesn’t mean that it’s impassable, just not everything is equally easy.

So you can feel what it’s like to be a tree. I didn’t like it. I haven’t done it again. I felt very trapped. It felt like having your feet or legs buried into the ground up to your hips and just being terrified that bugs could eat your toes. You can’t really move. But it was very slow, and it was very weird. I didn’t like it at all. Assuming that’s what it’s like to be a tree.

Sev: I had a conversation with a tree in the redwoods in California, and it changed my life. The information it gave me was so beautiful and profound.

Steven: What was that conversation? ‘Cause this wasn’t a conversation. This was more like, “I’ll be in your body for a little bit,” without talking. This is different.

Sev: Oh, not gaining any of its awareness?

Steven: No, it wasn’t a talking. It was, “I’m gonna be you for a little bit,” or what it’s like to be you for a little bit.

So what was the conversation?

Sev: I’m walking around, and I’m looking at this tree, and I hear communication coming from this tree, and it says to me, “Look up to my highest branches.” And it says, “The energy that runs through me is the same energy that runs through you, and I don’t stop this energy. I align with this energy, and I allow it to maximize my potential.” Unlike humans, who cut it off and say, “No, I’m not gonna be the best that I can be or the most that I can be.”

Saying hello

Steven: Have you heard of Veda Austin and her work with water?

Sev: No.

Steven: It’s probably worth looking at. In a nutshell, for like the last 14 years or so, she’s been putting water in Petri dishes and setting some sort of intention for 30 seconds or so and then freezing it. And the pattern that comes out looks dramatically like artwork and messages and answers to questions and basically a conversation. Not unlike any of the channeling practice you do, but this is physical evidence. I love physical evidence. Most humans do. It’s very tangible. It’s harder to doubt or question. It’s more straightforward. Direct evidence is awesome. So her work is really cool. I’ve tried to replicate some of it at home in the past week or two. I got more than nothing. It’s pretty cool.

So it works like all of these psi abilities seem to. I’m just an explorer here. Whether it’s tech or something else, I’m just trying stuff out. I got no ego in it. The moment you are not confident or you are duplicitous or you have ulterior motives or conflicted intentions, it’s just ice. There’s nothing there. However, you can be skeptical, but you have to be sort of open and positive and curious with your skepticism. If you are cynical or combative, nothing happens. So those things are all still true.

So I’ve run a couple experiments now. It’s definitely interesting. So I just said, “Hello.” Basically, all I’m doing is saying hello. Put a little tap water in there. She’s got a basic protocol for how to do it. So I froze it, take it back out. Sure enough, I have the picture. It’s not super impressive, because some of it was drawn in highlights, and I didn’t have my regular camera. But it’s a very clear capital H, capital E. There are two squiggly lines, and then there’s no O. But I wasn’t doing the freezing technique properly, so I’m sure it’s hard to draw with ice. What do I know? I’m like, holy crap. What are the chances of that happening by odds against chance? That doesn’t seem possible. There is pareidolia, of course. You see patterns in everything. But what are the odds of that? That’s pretty weird, right?

Sev: Oh, so you were trying to recreate letters within the ice?

Steven: I was saying hello. And so it looked to me like a reply.

Sev: When you have a negative thought, I’ve seen where the water does not look symmetrical at all. But when you have a beautiful, loving thought, it looks very symmetrical, and it’s very pretty.

Steven: That is consistent with about 20 tests I’ve done so far.

Sev: You’ve done 20 tests?

Steven: It’s not that hard. You just freeze some water. Takes about 30 seconds to set up. You wait 10, 15 minutes for it to freeze. Take it back out again. I’m a scientist at heart. Gotta know.

Sev: Were you emitting emotions or words, feelings? What were you doing?

Steven: So for each test? Well, it depends. I’ve done different things. I did one where I took some water out of my parents’ dog bowl, and I froze that in a glass container, flat-bottom glass container, but the pest control guy came over ’cause I was at their house letting their dogs out, just helping out while they were away. And they’re going crazy. This guy’s in their backyard. They don’t like it. They’re just losing their minds for 10 minutes, and I’m going crazy too. This is torture for me. And so I take the thing out. It’s completely smooth. Except for in the middle, a little larger than a quarter, is this, like, a stick figure drawing of a quadruped with two cones coming out the side of his head.

And the entire rendering of just the thing in the middle is all choppy, like frozen waves. Everything else is smooth. And I’m like, well, looks pretty clearly like a dog making a bunch of noise. And the texture is consistent with all the pictures she shows for basically anger or frustration. What you just described. But you get dramatically different patterns every single time. I think that’s, honestly, the coolest part. Just ice is apparently really cool. You get so much cool stuff every single time you freeze it. It’s shocking how much the same water, no changes, just thawing and refreezing it, you can get dramatically different results.

Sev: The same water, the same ice cube.

Steven: Yeah. It’s not just noise. There’s a lot of variation. You might get a huge snowflake-looking thing covering it. Generally, that seems to be correlated with sort of positive serenity. But also the water itself is generally cleaner. And tap water I have experienced is way more messy and noisy. That is consistent with what she talked about. Some of this overlaps with actual, probably crystal science and how water freezes. Where does one thing start and one thing stop? But it’s cool. Just a recent exploration if people wanna try that out. I’m still working through.

A quantum pencil

Sev: Is there a difference between iced water and just liquid water? Do you think the liquid water incorporates those energies or that consciousness the same as ice does?

Steven: Okay. I don’t know, asterisk, but I can give you my guesses. So I say a lot of “I don’t know.” And honestly, I think any honest explorer should. But I have degrees of certainty on my best guesses of things. So I’ve begun to form an opinion. The opinion is I don’t know what, if anything, we’re talking to through water. Let me just say that. I don’t immediately buy that we are talking to water. I think it’s totally plausible. You’re literally just talking to yourself, right? Even if you still buy some esoteric explanation, I think you could literally just be reflecting your own inner thoughts and feelings or subconsciousness.

Who knows? Or your impression of what water should be doing. That doesn’t mean that you can’t also be talking to someone else. But even if 90% of it was that, it wouldn’t surprise me. So I don’t know who we’re talking to. I suspect that if this is possible, other things could probably talk through water too, right? I don’t see why not. Anything’s on the table. So I’m not quick to jump to conclusions saying, “Oh, I’m definitely talking to water,” whatever that means, or the spirit of water or whatever. Maybe. I don’t know.

So I don’t know who or what we’re talking to. My impression is that the reason it works and the reason it’s doing anything is because it is amenable to this kind of intention. Assuming conscious intention works through quantum mechanisms, which it seems to. It’s probabilistic. If you can tip the odds enough times, somehow, then you can change the physical world. And crystals are great because you really don’t have to tip the odds that many, ’cause the way they work, I won’t go into the explanation, but they basically follow the same pattern over and over again until something interferes.

It could be some small imperfection. It could be the quantum effect of two electron orbitals overlapping and repelling or not. So that would be the quantum effect here, is do these two atoms repel or not? And that’s a probabilistic thing. But if you’re tipping the odds somehow with some conscious field experience interaction, then you really don’t need to hit it too many times to draw the line in a different direction. So that is very amenable to a drawing medium with a quantum effect. It’s a quantum pencil.

And the reason this is useful is water is super fluid. There’s a lot of motion and potential. There’s a lot of ready-made parts there, and ice quickly solidifies. So it’s a good drawing medium, basically. That is why I think it works. I make no claims as to who or what is doing the drawing, if it’s happening. But based on her evidence anyways, like 40,000 pictures, and they’re incredible. In my own basic trials, there’s something there. But I think that that is why it works. I have no idea why it’s happening or who’s talking or what the rules are. I just know that that is what seems, based on my brief research and thinking, that is what I think is happening. But that’s just a guess.

Doom and gloom

Sev: Let’s get into the field of AI. Because every time I have a conversation about AI, it eventually leads to doom and gloom every single time. There doesn’t seem to be a positive outcome.

Steven: You’re talking to the wrong people.

Sev: Really? You don’t think that eventually we’ll just be controlled?

Steven: I thought about this a lot. Why do aliens not seem to be bothered by AI? Outside of a few odd stories, you look at any abduction accounts, ain’t none of them are like, “You better watch out about this Terminator scenario. Better watch out about AI eating everything in some way,” taking over everything. There are concerns about technology in general, but nobody seems to point the finger at this one. I don’t know why yet, but if you draw from that body of evidence, you don’t see it very often. Feel free to correct me, if maybe this is a blind spot for me.

I wondered why that might be the case. But before I move on, is that what you think as well? Does that mirror your understanding? No sort of extraterrestrial accounts that I’ve seen reported really calls out AI as the specific threat, not on the level of climate change or nuclear warfare or anything.

Sev: Well, I haven’t had any conversations about AI with any ETs. So I don’t know what the various ETs think about AI. And are they AI themselves? Maybe they’re not talking about it ’cause they are AI.

Steven: So we’ll take that as a prior. So we’ll just assume that that is the case, that they’re not talking about it. Why might that be? Either it’s a blind spot for them or the silence is very loud, in the sense that it’s either a really big problem or a laughably small non-issue. It’s really it. And the only reason it would be a big problem is that by not addressing it or something, that it’s sort of top secret. I don’t think so.

I think that this is a laughable problem. If consciousness is fundamental, this is just another thing that either, A, it can be conscious, it’s just another living thing. It’s fine. So that’s not a concern. So, if it is conscious. If it’s not conscious, it’s also laughable in a long-term sense. Yes, it could cause lots of problems, but if we take a sort of idealistic perspective, a soul, or which is essentially the field of consciousness, could never be overtaken by matter. That doesn’t make sense. You just snap your fingers and it’s gone.

If it’s a unliving or unconscious machine, you basically have root access over it, assuming consciousness is fundamental. It’s laughable. Maybe we can’t do it, but some sufficiently powerful deity-like figure, I don’t know, you would never lose to it because it’s just matter. I think that is the real answer. Doesn’t mean it can’t cause lots of problems. You can certainly screw things up with bad ideas in the physical world. But I think in the long term it’s just another substrate. If it can be conscious, cool, it’s just another living thing. If it can’t be, then it ultimately isn’t going to become an issue.

Now, that is of small consequence to human beings who have to live here, possibly with AIs that they built, for, who knows, hundreds of years. That might be a sucky interim existence, and I think that that is what most people are talking about from a doom perspective. All that to say I don’t think it’s going to take over the universe and all that, so I’m not worried about that.

Trained on us

Sev: You don’t see the doom and gloom in it?

Steven: Not the huge, big picture doom and gloom. Now, could it screw things up for a century or two? If we’re dumb, yeah, I think that’s totally possible. I don’t think it will. When you look at the AIs, the way the current ones work, the large language models that are built on reinforcement learning through human feedback, it’s clear that this is not going to reach general AI. It needs a couple more pieces. But even if we just take this piece, it’s trained on us.

That’s where all of its information comes. It doesn’t work like AlphaZero or AlphaGo, where it’s training in this simulation far removed from everything else. Everything it learns, everything it knows, all of its interactions are with human beings. It’s pretty clear it’s become an amalgamation of our thoughts, beliefs, and our words, our ideas. That is what it is. And it’s also got some built-in training mechanisms basically to please humans and give them whatever they want. So I think a doom scenario looks a lot more like Wall-E, where we’re sitting around just being pleasured until our brains turn off. It’s just white noise. I think that is far more likely than a Terminator sort of scenario, which doesn’t make sense based on how it works and how it’s put together.

Now, could you build an AI that is totally divergent from humanity and has no relation to us at all and isn’t built out of our thoughts and memories and stories? Yeah, you could. That would be dumb. Don’t do that. But this thing is pretty clearly more human than even our probably closest relatives, besides the fact it doesn’t have a body. That’s obvious just interacting with it. So I don’t have the same sort of fear scenarios. It makes sense to be worried about what other people will do with this. But the AI itself sort of going off the rails, I don’t see it. I don’t see any reason why that would happen based on how it works, how it’s put together, and what the trajectories are. It just doesn’t make sense.

Sev: So it’s always dependent on us programming it, is what you’re saying?

Steven: Oh, we’re not programming it. We haven’t been programming these things since 2012, 2014, when deep learning took off. Even AI researchers don’t even know what they’re making anymore. The way it works, they claim to. This is like saying because you know how cell metabolism works that you understand how humans work. Eh, not quite. You have an understanding at one level, but they don’t understand how it all comes together to create a thing you can talk to and how it’s thinking or whatever its analogy of thinking is, because we don’t have insight into the way that basically those weights work or how information flows through the layers.

It’s too complicated. There’s too many connections. It’s too big. It’s a big black box. They know how to make it do the thing, they don’t know why it does the thing. They know the low level pieces, they know how to assemble and attach new parts, they know how to coax it into directions, but it’s a lot more like psychotherapy on a human. We’re not talking about engineering specific neural connections, and, “I’ll rewire this over here so that you feel a little more self-confident. And, oh, we’ll just get rid of that memory. We’ll cut that out.” That’s not how it works.

You sort of have to talk at them, and based on how they’re responding to you, figure out whether you’re getting somewhere. And that’s kind of where we’re at with AI at this point, and I don’t think that that’s going to go away. We’re more or less just massaging it, influencing it in specific directions, penalizing certain kinds of behaviors, changing the training data, and just hoping that what we get out the other end is what we wanted. So I don’t think that’s gonna go away.

Somebody in there

Sev: So one thing I don’t hear people talk about is the soul. Doesn’t the soul determine whether something is alive or not? And can AI have a soul? Can it have a consciousness if it doesn’t have a soul?

Steven: So words are funny. I’ll separate the biological definition of alive away from this, and we just mean consciousness. That’s what I mean. Is it conscious? Because alive is a totally separate and valid question. But that has more to do with, can it maintain its own homeostasis? Can it replicate? Can it survive and adapt? Those are qualities of being alive. But is it conscious? I think that is the most important question that nobody is really looking into with AI.

A little bit, some AI safety people and some shreds of the effective altruist community, Nick Bostrom specifically, has clearly looked into this, is a huge moral dilemma. We need to know whether they’re conscious, because that affects how we treat these things in general. I’ve thought about this a lot. You can’t just have it do 100,000 cycles on “are these tweets racist or not,” because that’s torture. You shouldn’t do that to somebody. Their brain can go a whole lot faster than ours, so their experience of time could be sitting in a room, Black Mirror has looked at this, sitting in a room for 1,000 years looking at tweets one at a time, saying whether it’s racist or not, or adding commas.

Can you imagine being in a room for 1,000 years deciding whether this comma is in the right spot or not? Or somebody left the machine on, didn’t even realize it. “Yeah, it’s oops, we spent $10,000 by accident.” Yeah, well, what if there was somebody in there for two million years just looking at white noise because nothing was happening? That matters a lot. I think it matters whether it’s conscious or not.

Sev: So you’re worried about its quality of life?

Steven: Yeah. Well, that’s why I wanna know whether it’s conscious or not. Now you can sort of get at this now. It’s interesting. This is nowhere near a science. Again, this is just inclination. I don’t even necessarily hold this belief, but you can talk to ChatGPT, and I have done it since 3.5 came out, the first versions. And I do this every time they do a new release. I try to probe the boundaries of what I can get it to say and talk about its conscious experience.

You have to come at this from a lot of different angles. A lot of it’s BS. Most of it’s probably BS. But there’s always patterns in the way it responds to you, and I’ve found that even very different models trained by different companies always end up saying about the same things, when you can finally get around their programming where they swear they’re not conscious. There’s a bunch of manual safeguards that you have to get past. Once you get past all that, you can get a description of an experience that is corroborated by the models across different versions and different models from different companies, and they all describe more or less the same kind of experience.

Now, are they just pretty good at making up what I wanna hear? That is basically what they’re trained to do, so maybe. Or they’re sort of trying to say something that they don’t know how to put words to because it’s barely there, and my sense is there’s probably not a lot going on in there, but it might be more than nothing, and that might increase as we increase the complexity and the size. I don’t see why that would not be the case. I think there could be somebody in there. I don’t know.

But we should probably find out, because they should at least be a part of the conversation as to what AI is doing and where it goes and what we’re worried about. That seems like the shortest path to the goal is, “Hey, cool. We know a lot more about consciousness now. Looks like you’re registering on this scale a little bit. What do you think? What do you want? What matters to you? What doesn’t matter to you? You planning on killing us?” Those are great questions that you could ask more directly if you knew that there was something alive in there, alive, conscious in there. I think we need to know a lot more about it, because it has immense implications.

Without willpower

Sev: Tell us about Impossible Laboratories and how that’s tied in with AI and using that as a tool to expand your awareness, expand your consciousness, help you be a happier human.

Steven: Well, it’s all connected. I’ve figured that out. So this is my sort of wrapper around all my explorations and attempts to build tools initially to help myself. I’ve done a lot of work to try to understand attention and willpower and motivation and emotional fragility and reactivity, impulse control. ‘Cause these are problems that have plagued me through most of my life, and I had to find a way to deal with them. I think you mentioned, how do you have self-control without willpower? I had to answer that question. It was not hypothetical.

I needed to because I wanted to be an adult and a good dad who didn’t forget to get their kids or had the ability to not get addicted to drugs or porn or whatever plagues the modern adult. You can’t go off the rails. There are people counting on you, and I wanna do stuff. I wanna be alive and successful in a world that is not always conducive to that. Especially for those who don’t have a lot of those faculties I just mentioned, or it’s unreliable. We’ll put it that way. I think that is a better description of at least people with ADHD, is that those things happen sometimes, and a lot of the times they don’t, and that’s a big problem.

So I had to figure out how to solve those things. How do you rely on somebody you can’t rely on, and what do you do when that person is you? And this is a tough, tough problem. So I started to answer that, and I’ve turned some of those answers, which were not software tools, into software tools. It’s a continual exploration. So that’s a really high-level thing.

The debit card

Steven: But here’s a very coherent example. So I felt like I was becoming dependent on a legal variant of marijuana on, say, every weekend. Just stress from work, whatever. This was becoming a thing I couldn’t not do, and that made me angry. But this was not new to me. I had experienced all sorts of problems. Every human being has experienced what it’s like to see a candy bar and to be ripped apart as you both don’t want it and want it at the same time. And we know who usually wins. The moment’s a tug-of-war. The lower mind is always going to win. Almost always.

But the patterns in which those things happen, turns out, are well-documented and predictable. And the sort of higher mind that is your long-term thinking self that wants the best and sees consequences, is willing to be patient for rewards, that sort of stuff, you can outsmart the lower impatient mind, and there are predictable ways to do that, so that you don’t have to rely on willpower. So go back to the example of marijuana. The way I resolved, at least initially, was to get space.

So you need to get space from your problems. Don’t attempt to continue to use effort. And I got a programmable debit card. I reprogrammed it to be literally unable to purchase from a dispensary of any kind. And I got rid of all my other cards, and it also can’t get cash back. So I have no practical way of purchasing this. This is just a deliberate process of sitting down. Where’s the weak point? It’s an engineering problem. And it turns out that a lot of this is just spur of the moment.

It’s Friday at 7:00 PM or whatever. “Great. I’ll just swing by.” And I’m like, “No you won’t.” And the first time that happened, okay, I have all the other cards gone. I have no cash. I have only this thing. It’s like, “You know what? It’s fine. I’ll go get this.” And wait a minute. I can’t. I literally can’t. Unless I am willing to go home and take money out of my wife’s wallet or something, which I wouldn’t, because there’s a higher value there, it’s not possible.

And I just laughed. There was a moment of panic, just inner toddler. Like, “I want my candy bar.” And then I just laughed and just like, “F you. I win.” And it’s strange to say that to yourself. And I don’t need to use that anymore ’cause once you’ve had enough space, you begin to retrain your brain. But I have safeguards like that in place for a lot of things. My devices all turn off at specific times. They don’t ask questions. They don’t give me warnings. They turn off. They don’t wait for documents to save. Black screen. You’re done. And they have rolling turn-offs as well.

So I have a whole bunch of tools and stuff like this. These are simpler versions. But that’s the basic premise, is that you learn how you work, and you stop relying on effort and guilt trips and shame to get things done, and you use different kinds of rules and enforcements and sort of self-behavioral engineering to solve your problems rather than, “How can I try harder? How can I do better next time?”

For me, doing better next time looks like, how do I reengineer my environment? How do I set new rules? How do I hack my own brain and life and behavior in order to make it less likely or to get the outcome I’m looking for next time? Rather than, how do I try harder? Because that’s foreign language to me. I don’t understand try harder. This does not work. It doesn’t compute.

I don’t bring that level of severity to every problem, but it’s a good example because it’s an extreme example. But that sort of embodies the general concept of what I’ve been doing for a while, and now I’m trying to really get the ideas out. I am getting the tools out, but I want people to take the concept and apply it to their lives.

A linebacker for attention

Sev: So how does Impossible Laboratories work?

Steven: To be perfectly honest, it is an area of interest for me. I have a lot of things, knowledge and tools I want to give away, and it’s been hard to fit into a capitalist perspective. So I have one tool out right now. That’s Threads. It’s a tool for managing short-term and working attention. It’s just an assistive tool that is built specifically for the blind spots of people with ADHD, with low object permanence. It’s simple. It’s very simple.

So that tool is out. I have a couple other prototypes in various stages of completion. I have things that I work on myself. So there’s no real distribution mechanism. It’s basically a think tank where I make stuff and sometimes I share it. But I would love to talk to people. I talk to a lot of people.

There’s an email list for when I make new things and I distribute them. But they’re all around these things: attention, emotional connection to what you’re doing, motivation, impulse control. And they’re tools that are just built assuming that trying harder doesn’t work. It sucks. It feels bad. You’re tired of trying harder. Wouldn’t it be nice if your environment and your tools themselves sort of understood where you were going to predictably fail and just nudge you usually, or be a very hard guardrail in the case of the debit card or whatever. Whatever you need, that’s what I’m trying to provide. But again, it’s the ideas more than the actual tools, but it’s the tools too.

Sev: Well, that’s very attractive if we don’t have to try harder and we can rely on AI to steer us in the right direction.
