Steven: That’s what got me excited a few years ago when I started, is that if my computer could know what I’m doing at all times, then I could change the way I interact with it. The punchline is that I’ve done a lot of other things. I don’t need that level of service anymore, which is amusing. But that’s fine. That is personal growth. That’s even a better outcome. But some people do. And I want them to be able to take hold of these ideas and tools and use them.

I don’t need most of them the same way I used to, fortunately. I found other paths to goal. But it’s still a meaningful idea that I think is a useful assistive tool to get to a better place where you don’t need them so much anymore, and we should all be changing our relationship with our devices. And AI is one tool. This is a positive use that I would love to see more of, which is essentially your computer working for you instead of against you, and more being like a linebacker for attention, helping to guide your attention and intention towards the things you actually care about, and sort of putting up friction when you’re doing the things that you know make you feel bad, that you truly don’t wanna do. That is still a big area of interest for me.

With no plan

Sev: How old do you think someone needs to be before they can start using tools like this?

Steven: Zero. If you’re interacting with technology, unfortunately you can’t be doing it with no plan anymore. This has become mainstream. Most people have figured out that their smartphone is not really for them. It’s not really on their side, and they should treat it with great skepticism. I’ve gone a good deal farther than that. So they’re not really built for you, and that’s a big problem with a lot of these things, is I’m talking about trying to slap an adapter onto something that is fundamentally designed to take your attention and put it to somebody else’s usually capitalistic ends that are generally not great for you.

And your attention and your intention, it’s just energy behind your attention, is really all you have. That’s the only real control you have in the world, that you can claim to have control at all. That’s it. If you don’t have attention or you don’t have the ability to put will towards something, you’ve got nothing. You’re out of control. You’re just on rails. You’re in somebody else’s story, whether you realize it or not. So I think that this is a fundamental problem, but nobody should be interacting with any form of technology, especially not smartphones or tablets, with no plan. I’ve dramatically changed with the way I interact with technology. Even as a technologist, it looks very different from what the average person does.

Sev: How is it different? What do you do that’s different than maybe how I interact with my phone?

Steven: Well, I haven’t had a smartphone for a long time. I have one temporarily now ’cause my dumb phone broke. I had a Light Phone, which is more or less a flip phone. It does calls, it does texts. That’s it. It doesn’t do anything else. I don’t want it to do anything else. My computers all turn off at specific times. I have very deliberate rules. When I play video games, they’re on hard timers. Anything that could pull me in can’t. There are no leads, so I’ve sort of buried the leads to a lot of these things.

I have no notifications of any kind. No sounds. The only way to actually reach me is to make a phone call. So text messages don’t make sounds. There’s no notification dots. It’s all a blank slate. All of these things are redesigned in such a way that, A, the only time I use them is when I initiate the action from something internal. So it’s not something else is pulling me in. And the second is that they can’t keep me pulled in. I have firewall blockers that make specific kinds of websites very difficult for me to go to.

Any kind of social media, that is all blocked in like three different ways. I can undo it, but it takes five to 10 minutes to undo it all. And guess what? When you’re sitting at home at 5:00 after work, turns out you don’t wanna do 10 minutes of little system admin work to undo your safeguards to go look at Reddit. Eh, whatever. You just don’t. You close your computer and you go walk outside. That seems like a small thing, but that’s how it works.

There’s no icons. There’s no way for anything to pull me in. Nothing happens automatically. It’s just I open my computer, it’s quiet, it’s still. It waits for me to do things. There’s no ads. Everything is blocked. There’s nothing. Nothing. There’s no news. No news. Absolutely no news anywhere. Infotainment is horrific for what it does to us. I don’t have any of this. As a result, I don’t have any of the craving or needing habits to make myself feel better or just because I’m interested, to go open these devices and go do those things, because I’ve deadened those responses.

So I have a different relationship with devices. They’re just tools again now. For the most part. I’m a human being and I have flaws like anybody, but there’s enough friction there.

The correct use for willpower

Sev: Well, that takes a lot of willpower.

Steven: It does. And that is the correct use for willpower, is to design strategies and systems to stop using it. That is, in fact, the only good use for willpower when it comes to solving problems like this, is willpower is not something you should rely on. That is the approach I have taken, and that has worked way better. Willpower is for picking yourself back up and deciding how you’re going… I have a rule for that. It’s literally in my list of rules that I review every morning.

As nerdy as that sounds, I have a spreadsheet that is my rules and systems. I review it every morning. It’s a master habit. And one of them is, I close the door behind me after making a bad choice. So if I break one of my rules that matters, the only penance I have to make up for it is just make it harder. Just add a little bit of friction. Just make it a little bit harder to not repeat that thing.

Whatever it was. You stayed up until 2:00 AM watching anime. Cool. No problem. All is forgiven. The only thing you have to do in penance is make it a little bit harder to repeat that same bad behavior next time, and we’re good. And just by applying that over and over again, and that is the correct use for willpower, is, how do I make this a little harder? So now I have adapters that plug into my wall, and you have to push the button for a specific amount of time before the entire TV turns off, the whole wall outlet turns off.

So I press one hour, and then you’re sitting there. The whole room goes dark. Yes, I can get back up and press the button and wait for it to turn back on, find the channel. You’re not gonna. You’re gonna go to bed. So that’s it. That’s how everything changed. It looks like a lot from the outside, and I’m not covering probably 100 different things, but that’s how it all came about. It’s just that simple principle.

Sev: You can only try so hard and you can’t try any harder. And then the frustration comes in that I’m not trying hard enough, but you’re trying as hard as you can humanly try. And then it’s not good enough. And then you just spiral. And so what you’re doing is you’re preventing people from going into that spiral.

Steven: That spiral is actually self-indulgent, as strange as that sounds. It is re-enabling. You get it. I know you do. You’ve given me the advice not to do that. But it is self-perpetuating. If I were to stay up till 2:00 AM to watch anime, I have to get up early to go to work or whatever, I’m angry. That anger, that sort of self-loathing is fuel to recreate that same cycle again. You get more of it, not less of it, and it’s misdirected. To take a sort of Daoist point of view, you are creating a whiplash effect.

When you beat yourself up like that, you are now more fragile. You trust yourself less. You’re more stressed out. And what do you do when you feel bad and you’re stressed out and you’re angry? You make more bad choices, like staying up late and watching anime, and you’re gonna do it again. And you’re gonna hate yourself even more until it becomes a complex. It’s not enough to just let go. You also have to do something about it, but with a cool head.

On a whim

Sev: Now, what made you reach out and connect with me a year or two ago?

Steven: I don’t know. I will shorthand it to intuition, and since then I have tried to learn how to trust that sense a lot more, as a directive force, on a long timeline in life, to know what steps to take or what matters. I think I heard you in an interview. It may have been with Mike. I can’t remember.

Sev: Mike Clelland, the owl guy?

Steven: Yeah. I think so. I can’t remember, though. And I just, on a whim, I’m like, “You know, I’m gonna call her,” or, “I’m gonna email her and see if she does sessions or coaching or something.” I don’t know why. I just feel like at the very least it’s gonna be funny and, no offense, she’ll be a kook, and it’ll be a great story. Or, I did come in sincerely, like, “But I hope it’s useful and interesting and I grow in some way.” I definitely came in skeptical but sort of positive and hoping for the best. You never know what you’re gonna get.

So I am a client multiple times with you. I consider you to be a friend from afar and one of many coaches. And I hope to continue so. But yeah, I think we did the soul purpose session first. I pulled it up here, ’cause I wanted to talk about it because it had a profound effect on my life.

So we’ve done a couple since. But I have the first one pulled up in front of me. I was in a place where I had figured out a lot of ways to be good, at the small scale, on the daily life sort of way. I knew how to manipulate myself and engineer my life and environment to be good or to be relatively stable, or I found out how to be happy. I had figured that out. Took a long time. It took a number of years, but I found out how to be happy on a pretty reliable basis or to get back to that place quickly.

But I didn’t know what to do with my life. Job pretty good, relationship pretty good. Everything’s in place, but meaning and purpose is definitely lacking. I wanna do something, but I don’t know what. I have all this energy. I don’t know where to put it. And I feel like I should be using it. Needed some sort of high-level guidance. So that’s what this was all about.

Math and magic

Steven: I’m looking at what you wrote down in the session. And it was very profound, and it changed the course of what I explored. I wouldn’t say that I thought channeling was BS before we met. I felt that if there were any, there probably weren’t very many that were qualified. Most were charlatans, and that there probably wasn’t a lot of information there. But it was just spot on. Really, really eerie. From the first sentence. The first sentence is, “This one can smell BS a mile away.”

I just couldn’t help but laugh, and that is exactly what I was thinking. “Don’t ask him if you don’t want to know the truth.” That is word for word, and we did basically no interaction before talking. You didn’t want to interact with me at all.

Sev: I did not want to.

Steven: And this whole thing was not only a perfect description of how I felt and my approach to life and what I felt I was good at, but it was clearly from a third-party lens that I won’t say didn’t care about me, but we’ll say is objective. It’s unbiased. I felt I was getting the unvarnished truth, good or bad. So in a way that I would not have described myself but wouldn’t disagree with. And I got a lot of high-level direction.

And more than that, I know it sounds silly, but a lot of what I learned I would have known even then, but the problem, especially with me, is not coming up with ideas of what to do. It is having the sort of self-trust and certainty that what I’ve decided is the right one. Or believing in the ideas or thoughts I’ve already had. And a lot of this was reaffirming that, okay, well, now that I’ve heard it from the outside, the far outside, who am I to say no? To have asked for help and to be given new answers for sure, but also reaffirming ones.

And that really is what gave me the confidence that there was really something here. Of course, I had already had a UFO sighting, so I knew that I was already forcing myself to be open and humble. But it was the combination of new and reaffirming information that told me, “Dang, there’s something here.” This was really helpful, and she knows what she’s talking about. You also just give pretty good advice yourself.

I think it was money well spent to answer one of the most profound and really painful problems in my life, which is, what am I doing here? I wanted to decide. I didn’t wanna be told. I don’t like to be told what to do, but at the same time, I want some direction, and I want to know the potential and the options and where I’m falling short and where I’m psyching myself out. And it was incredibly valuable to find out from an outside source. You’d call it a second opinion. Second opinion with a lot of very different perspective from your own, I think, is a good way of looking at this. Because it does care about you.

Here, I’m gonna pull up the punchline, and then we’ll leave it there. Not that there was only one punchline. I’ll have to do it from memory, ’cause I can’t find the exact quote. Here it is. “I’m here more or less to help humans realize their nature as math and magical beings.” Something along those lines. I don’t have the exact quote here.

Sev: Math?

Steven: Yeah. Basically, we’re all just made of math and magic, and I’m… just showed up here to help people realize that. It’s really vague, right? But what a perfect description. I never would’ve used those words, and yet the moment I heard it, I’m like, “That’s all-encompassing. That is a perfect way of putting this.” And that’s what I’m all about, math and magic. I insist on testing weird stuff, but also accountability to skepticism and humility and some openness at the same time, I think. I wanna bring those together, and it really set me on that path, and I’m having a lot of fun, and I’m excited to see where it goes.

Running out of time

Sev: So you’re not on Instagram or YouTube or… Yeah, you’re not.

Steven: What do you think?

Sev: This could easily go for another hour. I really don’t wanna stop it here, but I’m running out of time, ’cause we haven’t even touched on your UFO story.

Steven: Or remote viewing or anything.

Steven: That would be my perspective as an intermediate practitioner. What was it like to go from know nothing to intermediate? I don’t claim to be a master, but I think that’s useful for people who are just beginners.

Sev: And you think everybody can do it?

Steven: Absolutely. It has a lot of overlap with your channeling techniques. There’s a lot of overlap with how it works. It’s a different approach. We’ll call it, like, 60 to 70% related. There’s a lot of cross-training.

Sev: Are we plugging into that same conscious field? Is that what’s going on?

Steven: Pretty same underlying methodology. You’re just working with it in a different way, trying to do different things.

How to try it yourself

The series set out to give, as he says in the trailer, everything he knew about how to do it yourself. This is an index of the practices, instructions and cautions he gives, in the order they appear. The labels are the editors’; follow each link for what he actually says, including his warnings about who should not try.

  • Daily meditation, twice a day
  • Recommended reading: The Untethered Soul

  • Setting goals by present values rather than future outcomes
  • Testing unusual claims first-hand
  • Drawing and journaling an experience immediately
  • Seeking it out yourself and persisting
  • Taking an observer perspective to stay grounded
  • Ruling out other explanations, including psychiatric help
  • Psi wheel training for telekinesis
  • Daily remote viewing practice
  • Caution: never look at the sun with open eyes

  • Entertaining the inner conversation as a pretense
  • Holding a belief until it happens
  • Asking for real-world validation
  • Trusting non-physical senses
  • Leaning into imagination and belief as steering
  • Keeping a positive orientation and continuing
  • Asking for your own version and following breadcrumbs

What I Know About Transcendence

  • Get to a stable place in your life before starting
  • Decide for yourself what it all means
  • Be authentic to yourself
  • Read The Untethered Soul (or the audiobook)
  • Entertain an unlikely idea long enough to test it
  • Seeking: set an intention to know the answers to your big questions
  • Sustain the desire through periods of no results
  • Read the Yoga Sutras in a translation with commentary
  • Meditate every day for at least 30 minutes
  • Beginning meditation: think of nothing and let thoughts slide off
  • Non-dual meditation; the Waking Up app and its courses
  • His own contemplative meditation: let go over and over until the mind is still
  • Pay attention to your own state during the day
  • Use your intuition: look for a feeling in everyday life and follow it
  • Two-week test of living by intuition instead of planning
  • See meaning in everything that happens and ask why it happened
  • Notice synchronicities, acknowledge them, and decide what they mean
  • Treat the problem in front of you as the right problem and ask what it is teaching
  • Practice the skills that appear spontaneously to bring them under control
  • With non-physical encounters, hold a strength of self
  • Treat small annoyances as serious problems with a lesson
  • What not to do when anomalous events occur
  • Ask what happened and decide what it means yourself
  • Update your beliefs and world models to incorporate the experience
  • Act as if the experience is true
  • Talk it out with a support group or in a safe space
  • Learn how to process trauma; mind how you encode the memory
  • Sit with a difficult experience until it is fully worked through
  • Rest and recover before going on
  • Stay connected to your ordinary life and responsibilities
  • Do not stay in victimhood or act inauthentically
  • Keep applying the lessons you have already learned
  • Hold open the belief that something can be true
  • Face your fears, insecurities and old traumas
  • Expect the experiences to be absurd or confusing
  • Set an intention as a request, mean it and live it
  • When nothing seems to happen, examine the lesson in front of you
  • Practice with other people; go to training events free from dogma
  • Cut your information diet to almost zero
  • Notice what leaves you feeling worse and let go of crutches
  • Stop judging and blaming yourself
  • Lean into your life rather than away from it
  • Find a community so you are not alone in it
  • Do not push this on people who are not ready
  • Help other people

Self-Control and Mental Clarity

  • Send an AI ahead of you to filter what you are exposed to online
  • Set your own rules for life and have something watch and enforce them
  • Reach podcasts, the web and social media through an interface that does not pull your attention
  • State your intentions and be made aware when what you are doing does not match
  • Choose escalating restrictions for yourself in advance
  • A hard guardrail for substance abuse: deny the transaction
  • Add friction, or remove what makes life worse, instead of using willpower
  • Decide what to access and become aware of what is influencing you
  • Notice what makes you feel bad, then remove or blunt it
  • Make what is bad for you hard and what is good for you easy
  • Stay a little behind the cutting edge and try the tools
  • Ask what automation is replacing in your life
  • Ask what you are freeing up time and energy for

The Technology That Centers You

  • Keep only what you are doing now and next in front of you, to get it out of your head
  • A daily 30-minute walk as an approachable alternative to meditation
  • Thirty minutes a day of walking or sitting bored, in silence, doing nothing
  • The chair test
  • Sit in the chair and feel the discomfort until it subsides
  • Stop the stimulation for a while and let yourself crash
  • Return to zero faster by deliberately becoming uncomfortable
  • Change your balance point by changing what you do
  • Review your own principles and rules for life every morning
  • Write down a life lesson when something important happens, and reread it
  • Put the state you want to be in at the center of your life

Human and Machine Consciousness

  • Daily meditation, about an hour every morning
  • Using a session to reset when angry or stressed
  • Letting go: whatever attention is on is not the thing
  • Progressive separation from sensations, emotions and thoughts (“that’s not me”)
  • Learning from a tradition by asking why it works and trying it yourself
  • Increasing sitting time gradually
  • Sitting a few hours after waking rather than straight away
  • Picking a technique or target of focus and trying it out
  • Rules and systems in place of willpower

Alien Spirit

  • Sitting meditation (his own practice is about an hour each morning)
  • Single-object focus meditation: staring at a tree for an hour
  • Approaching psi experiments with open, positive skepticism
  • Freezing water after setting an intention (Veda Austin’s protocol)
  • Get space from a problem instead of using effort: the programmable debit card
  • Devices that shut off at set times without warning
  • Re-engineer the environment and rules instead of trying harder
  • A calls-and-texts-only phone and hard timers on games
  • No notifications; use devices only when you initiate it
  • Block social media behind several minutes of friction
  • No ads and no news on his devices
  • Review a written list of rules and systems every morning
  • Close the door behind you: after a lapse, add friction instead of penance
  • Outlet timers that cut power to the TV
  • Don’t beat yourself up; act on the problem with a cool head

Sources and editorial note

This is a reading edition of fourteen recordings preserved in the Steven Schrembeck Archive. Thirteen were transcribed by machine with ElevenLabs Scribe v2 on October 10, 2026. The fourteenth, from The Grateful Podcast, uses the transcript its publisher supplied, which does not label speakers; speakers there were identified from context. No transcript has had a full listening review, and the source files are unchanged.

Each paragraph was checked by program against its transcript: its words must all occur, in the same order, in the transcript of the named speaker, starting at the place the manuscript cites. The check compares words only. Case, punctuation, quotation marks and paragraphing are editorial. Transcript spellings of five names were corrected: Steven, Schrembeck, Swenson, Estes, and Nom. The corrections are counted in the validation record. Matching words in order shows that nothing was added. It does not prove the machine heard each word correctly, and it does not make a selection neutral.

A companion file, the passage map, lists every paragraph with its recording, speaker, start and end times, the original transcript wording, and the edited wording. The manuscripts, the checker and the builder are kept with the archive so the edition can be rebuilt and re-verified.

The notes below give, for each chapter, its recording, the date the archive has for it, how much of each speaker’s transcribed speech the chapter retains, and what was left out.

Invitation

The complete trailer. One filler word was removed.

Back-channel responses, filler, false starts and immediate repetitions were removed throughout, and Joshua’s longer reflections were condensed by cutting. Nothing Steven recounts or explains was left out.

Recordings not represented

The prologue was first published on Joshua Swenson’s podcast Ruptures and Roots as “Beyond Logic: Steven Schrembeck on Mind Sight, Soul Families, and the Edges of Consciousness.” The archive holds that recording too. It is the same conversation and appears here once.

Steven’s 2023 interview on The CTO Show with Mehmet Gonullu concerns building products with AI and is not represented. Two further guest appearances listed on his speaking profile, on The Creative Mindset and The Podcast Inside Your House, could not be recovered for the archive. His audio fiction, his videos and his workplace talks are outside the scope of this book.

Dates

Dates for the series are the publication dates in its podcast feed, not recording dates. The feed dates the three journals October 1 to 3, 2026. Steven’s death in September 2026 is recorded in the memorial written by his friend Jeff Cameron. The epigraph is the line Steven placed at the top of his speaking profile, preserved in the archive.

Further reading

Steven wrote about the same experiences and ideas in essays at his site A Different Compass. The archive preserves them, among them “Reality 102: Souls,” “See,” “How to know,” “To Know,” “Feel it first,” “How to get impossible things faster,” and “The Gift of Everything.” A shorter compiled book, The Small Moments Are Everything, draws selections from across his recordings, including his talks on work and meaning.
